Ergodicity of the geodesic flow for groups with a contracting element
Rémi Coulon

Abstract
In this article we investigate the dynamical properties of the geodesic flow for a proper metric space whose isometry group contains a contracting element. We show that the existence of a contracting isometry is a sufficient evidence of negative curvature to carry in this context various results borrowed from hyperbolic geometry. In particular, we extend the Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy proving that the geodesic flow is either conservative and ergodic or dissipative.



Keywords:

negatively curved groups, contracting element, geodesic flow, conservativity, ergodicity.

Primary MSC Classification:

20F65, 20F67 37A10, 37A15, 37A25, 37D40.

1
Introduction

1.1
Background

Given a hyperbolic manifold
M=\mathbf{H}^{n}/G
, the geodesic flow
(\phi_{t})
on its unit tangent bundle
SM
is a fundamental example of dynamical system with various remarkable applications to geometry, topology, group theory and number theory.
It comes with a flow-invariant measure
m_{G}
, called the Bowen-Margulis measure (it may have different names in the litterature).
If
m_{G}
is finite, this measure is characterized as the unique invariant measure with maximal entropy.
The Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy states that two mutually exclusive cases can arise.
Either the geodesic flow is conservative (almost every orbit is recurrent) and ergodic or it is dissipative (almost every orbit is divergent).

Consider now a general proper geodesic metric space
X
endowed with a proper action by isometries of a group
G
.
As
X
does not necessarily carry a riemannian structure, a natural replacement for the unit tangent bundle of
M=X/G
is the quotient
\mathcal{G}X/G
, where
\mathcal{G}X
is the space of all bi-infinite, unit-speed parametrized geodesics on
X
.
It comes with a flow
(\phi_{t})
which consists in shifting the origin of the geodesics.
It turns out that a Hopf-Tsuji-Sulllivan dichotomy often holds as soon as
X
carries a certain form of negative curvature.
This is for instance the case if

—

X
is a tree, see Coornaert-Papadopoulos
[
8
]
;

—

X
is a Gromov hyperbolic space, see Kaimanovich
[
15
]
for a first proof with additional assumptions, and Bader-Furman
[
2
]
as well as Coulon-Dougall-Schapira-Tapie
[
10
]
for the general case;

—

X
is CAT(-1), see Burger-Mozes
[
6
]
and Roblin
[
23
]
;

—

X
is a geodesically complete CAT(0) space and
G
contains a rank-one isometry, see Ricks
[
22
]
for proper and co-compact actions, Link-Picaud
[
18
]
for Hadamard manifolds, and Link
[
17
]
for the general case.

In a similar spirit, we should also mention that for moduli spaces, the ergodicity of the Teichmüller flow has been proved independently by Veech
[
28
]
and Masur
[
19
]
.

Remark
.

The Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy also admits generalizations in a higher rank setting, see Burger-Landesberg-Lee-Oh
[
5
]
.
However for the purpose of this article we will remain in the context of “rank-one” spaces.
∎

1.2
Contracting elements

In the past decades, there have been many attempts to investigate the marks of negative curvature in groups, beyond hyperbolicity.
Contracting elements is a useful notion in this direction, see Bestvina-Fujiwara
[
3
]
.
Roughly speaking a subset
Y\subset X
is contracting, if any ball disjoint from
Y
has a nearest-point projection onto
Y
, whose diameter is uniformly bounded.
An element
g\in G
is
contracting
if the orbit map
\mathbf{Z}\to X
sending
n
to
g^{n}x
is a quasi-isometric embedding with contracting image.
Contracting elements can be thought of as hyperbolic directions in the space
X
.
Here are a few examples.

—

If
X
is a hyperbolic space (in the sense of Gromov) endowed with a proper action of
G
, then every loxodromic element in
G
is contracting
[
13
]
.

—

Assume that
G
is hyperbolic relative to
\{P_{1},\dots,P_{m}\}
.
Suppose that
G
acts properly, co-compactly on
X
.
Any infinite order element of
G
which is not conjugated in some
P_{i}
is contracting
[
25
,
12
]
.

—

If
X
is a CAT(0) space endowed with a proper, co-compact action of
G
, then any rank-one element of
G
is contracting
[
3
]
.

—

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented compact surface.
Assume that
G
is the mapping class group of
\Sigma
and
X
the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
endowed with the Teichmüller metric.
Then any pseudo-Anosov element is contracting
[
20
]
.

Groups with a contracting elements are known to be acylindrically hyperbolic, see Sisto
[
26
]
.

1.3
Main result

The goal of this article is to extend the Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy, whenever
G
admits a contracting element for its action on
X
, thus providing a common approach for the aforementioned examples.
In order to state precisely the main result, let us introduce first some important objects.
We assume here that
X
is a proper geodesic metric space and
G
a group acting properly, by isometries on
X
with a contracting element.
In addition, we suppose that
G
is not virtually cyclic.

The abstract space of the geodesic flow.

The
horocompactification
of
X
, denoted by
\bar{X}
, is the smallest compactification of
X
such that the map
X\times X\times X\to\mathbf{R}
sending
(x,y,z)
to
\mathchoice{d\left(x,z\right)}{d(x,z)}{d(x,z)}{d(x,z)}-\mathchoice{d\left(y,z\right)}{d(y,z)}{d(y,z)}{d(y,z)}
extends continuously to a map
X\times X\times\bar{X}\to\mathbf{R}
.
The horoboundary
\partial X=\bar{X}\setminus X
can be seen as a set of cocycles
c\colon X\times X\to\mathbf{R}
, generalizing the notion of Busemann cocycles.

Remark
.

If
X
is CAT(0), then its horoboundary coincides with the visual boundary.
If
X
is Gromov hyperbolic, then its Gromov boundary is homeomorphic to
\partial X/\negthickspace\sim
.
∎

We now consider the sets

\displaystyle\partial^{2}X

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\left\{(c,c^{\prime})\in\partial X\times\partial X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}<\infty\right\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime})\in\partial X\times\partial X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}<\infty\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime})\in\partial X\times\partial X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}<\infty\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime})\in\partial X\times\partial X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}<\infty\}},

\displaystyle SX

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}X\times\mathbf{R},

(the notation
\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{o}}
stands for the Gromov product of
c
and
c^{\prime}
at a point
o\in X
– see
Section 3.3
– in particular if
(c,c^{\prime})
belongs to
\partial^{2}X
, then
c
and
c^{\prime}
are distinct).
Both spaces come with a natural action of
G
, induced by the one on
X
.
One also defines a flow
(\phi_{t})
on
SX
by translating the coordinate in the
\mathbf{R}
factor.
This flow commutes with the action of
G
.
In addition, there is a natural
G
-invariant, flow-invariant map
\mathcal{G}X\to SX
.

Remark
.

If
X
is a geodesically complete, simply-connected manifold with negative sectional curvature, then
SX
is homeomorphic to the unit tangent bundle of
X
.
More generally, if
X
is CAT(-1) then the map
\mathcal{G}X\to SX
is a homeomorphism.
This identification is known as the Hopf coordinates.
∎

In our settings, the map
\mathcal{G}X\to SX
need not be onto (not any two points in the boundary are joined by a geodesic) nor one-to-one (see
Remark 6.2
).
Note also that
X
is not assumed to be geodesically complete.
Nevertheless it turns out that the flow
(\phi_{t})
on
SX/G
is an efficient analogue of the geodesic flow on a hyperbolic manifold.

Bowen-Margulis measure.

Now that we have described the spaces, let us explain what measure we will consider.
We follow a classical construction due to Patterson and Sullivan
[
21
,
27
]
which has been extended by Knieper in the CAT(0) setting
[
16
]
.
Fix a base point
o\in X
.
In a previous work
[
9
]
we built
G
-invariant, conformal densities
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
supported on
\partial X
(see
Section 3.6
).
First we form a
G
-invariant current
\mu
on
\partial^{2}X
which is absolutely continuous with respect to the product measure
\nu_{o}\otimes\nu_{o}
.
Then we endow
SX
with the measure
m=\mu\otimes ds
where
ds
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbf{R}
.
Finally we consider the sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}_{G}
which consists of all
G
-invariant Borel subsets and derive from
m
a measure
m_{G}
on
\mathfrak{L}_{G}
which plays the role of the measure on the quotient space
SX/G
.
The latter is our Bowen-Margulis measure.

However, it turns out that the space
SX
is too large for our purpose.
Assume for instance that the product
X=\mathbf{H}^{2}\times\mathchoice{\left[0\,,1\right]}{[0\,,1]}{[0\,,1]}{[0\,,1]}
is endowed with the
L^{1}
-metric and
G
is a group acting properly by isometries on
\mathbf{H}^{2}
and trivially on
\mathchoice{\left[0\,,1\right]}{[0\,,1]}{[0\,,1]}{[0\,,1]}
.
For every
u\in\mathchoice{\left[0\,,1\right]}{[0\,,1]}{[0\,,1]}{[0\,,1]}
, the “layer”
\mathbf{H}^{2}/G\times\{u\}\subset SX/G
is flow invariant.
Thus if our measure can distinguish between these layers, one cannot expect to prove that the geodesic flow is ergodic.
Already the horoboundary
\partial X
is not suitable for the
G
-invariant, conformal densities to behave nicely – see the discussion in
[
9
]
.
Motivated by the analogy with Gromov hyperbolic spaces, one could be tempted to replace everywhere
\partial X
by
\partial X/\negthickspace\sim
, where
\sim
is the equivalence relation identifying two cocycles
c_{1},c_{2}\in\partial X
whenever
\mathchoice{\left\|c_{1}-c_{2}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}<\infty
.
However, as a topological space, the quotient
\partial X/\negthickspace\sim
can be rather wild (it does not have to be Hausdorff for instance).
For this reason, we adopt a
measurable
point of view.
We denote by
\mathfrak{B}
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\bar{X}
and by
\mathfrak{B}^{+}
the sub-
\sigma
-algebra which consists of all Borel subsets which are saturated for the equivalence relation
\sim
.
We think of the
reduced horoboundary
as the measurable space
(\partial X,\mathfrak{B}^{+})
.
When restricted to
\mathfrak{B}^{+}
,
G
-invariant, conformal densities display many features which are typical from hyperbolic geometry, see Coulon
[
9
]
.
A similar phenomenon arises for the geodesic flow.
More precisely we endow
SX
with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{L}^{+}
which is the product of
\mathfrak{B}^{+}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{+}
and the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbf{R}
, and then define
\mathfrak{L}_{G}^{+}
as the collection of all
G
-invariant subsets in
\mathfrak{L}^{+}
.
The dynamical system we are really interested in is the geodesic flow
(\phi_{t})
acting on
(SX,\mathfrak{L}_{G}^{+},m_{G})
.

The Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy.

We are now in a position to state our main result.
Recall that the
Poincaré series
of the group
G
is

\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)=\sum_{g\in G}e^{-s\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}}.

Its
critical exponent
is denoted by
\omega_{G}
.
It has the property that
\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)
converges ( respectively diverges) whenever
s>\omega_{G}
( respectively
s<\omega_{G}
).
We say that the action of
G
on
X
is
divergent
if
\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)
diverges at
s=\omega_{G}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
X
be a proper, geodesic, metric space.
Fix a base point
o\in X
.
Let
G
be a group acting properly, by isometries on
X
.
We assume that
G
is not virtually cyclic and contains a contracting element.
Let
\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Let
\nu=(\nu_{x})
be a
G
-invariant,
\omega
-conformal density supported on
\partial X
.
Let
m_{G}
be the associated Bowen-Margulis measure on
(SX,\mathfrak{L}_{G})
.
Then one of the following two cases holds.
Moreover, for each one all the stated properties are equivalent.

—

Convergent case.

(c1)

The Poincaré series
\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)
converges at
s=\omega
.

(c2)

The measure
\nu_{o}
gives zero measure to the radial limit set.

(c3)

The diagonal action of
G
on
(\partial X\times\partial X,\mathfrak{B}\otimes\mathfrak{B},\nu_{o}\otimes\nu_{o})
is not conservative.

(c4)

The geodesic flow on
(SX,\mathfrak{L}_{G},m_{G})
is dissipative.

—

Divergent case.

(d1)

The Poincaré series
\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)
diverges at
s=\omega
, hence
\omega=\omega_{G}
.

(d2)

The measure
\nu_{o}
gives full measure to the radial limit set.

(d3)

The diagonal action of
G
on
(\partial X\times\partial X,\mathfrak{B}\otimes\mathfrak{B},\nu_{o}\otimes\nu_{o})
is conservative.

(d4)

The geodesic flow on
(SX,\mathfrak{L}_{G},m_{G})
is conservative.

(d5)

The diagonal action of
G
on
(\partial X\times\partial X,\mathfrak{B}^{+}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o}\otimes\nu_{o})
is ergodic.

(d6)

The geodesic flow on
(SX,\mathfrak{L}^{+}_{G},m_{G})
is ergodic.

If
X
is a CAT(0) space, then the (regular) horoboundary and the reduced horoboundary coincide.
Hence we recover the work of Link
[
17
]
.
Actually, in the divergent case, we even get a slightly more general result (see below).

Let
\Sigma
be a compact orientable surface of genus at least two.
Assume that
X
is the Teichmüller space of
\Sigma
endowed with the Teichmüller metric.
Recall that every pseudo-Anosov element in the mapping class group
G=\operatorname{MCG}(\Sigma)
is contracting for its action on
X
.
Thus our work applies in this situation as well.
Arzhantseva, Cashen and Tao
[
1
, Section 10]
also observed that the work of Eskin, Mirzakani, and Rafi
[
11
, Theorem 1.7]
implies that the action of
G
on
X
is strongly positively recurrent (a.k.a. statistically convex co-compact).
Consequently the Poincaré series
\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)
diverges at the critical exponent
s=\omega_{G}
– see Yang
[
29
]
for a geometric proof or Coulon
[
9
]
for a more dynamical proof inspired by Schapira-Tapie
[
24
]
.
Hence we recover the ergodicity of the Teichmüller flow.

1.4
Strategy

Let us now say a few words about our strategy.
Although the main scheme follows classical arguments, there are various (sometimes subtle) difficulties that needed be overcome.
In our previous work
[
9
]
we proved the equivalences
(d1)

\Leftrightarrow

(d2)
and
(c1)

\Leftrightarrow

(c2)
.
This part has been obtained independently by Yang
[
30
]
.
The relation with conservativity / dissipativity is rather standard — there are some technicalities that need be addressed though, such as the study of atoms in
(\partial X,\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
.

The Hopf argument.

Our main contribution is the ergodicity of the geodesic flow.
The proof follows the famous Hopf argument
[
14
]
.
If
X=\mathbf{H}^{n}
is the hyperbolic space, this approach can be summarized as follows.
Fix a positive summable function
\rho\in L^{1}(SX/G,m_{G})
.
Let
f\in L^{1}(SX/G,m_{G})
.
If the geodesic flow is conservative, then the Hopf ergodic Theorem states that for almost every vector
v\in SX/G
, the limit

\lim_{T\to\pm\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}f\circ\phi_{t}(v)dt}{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\rho\circ\phi_{t}(v)dt}

exists and defines a flow-invariant function
f_{\infty}
on
SX/G
.
Geodesics in
\mathbf{H}^{n}
satisfy a
convergence property
: if
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are two geodesic rays whose endpoints at infinity coincide, then there is
u\in\mathbf{R}
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathchoice{d\left(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t+u)\right)}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t+u))}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t+u))}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(t+u))}=0

(the convergence is even exponentially fast).
This feature can be used to prove that if
f
is continuous with compact support, then
f_{\infty}(v)
actually only depends on the “future” of
v
and by symmetry only on its “past”.
As
m_{G}
is built from a product measure on
\partial^{2}X
, one deduces with a Fubini argument that
f_{\infty}
is essentially constant.
Since continuous functions with compact support are dense in
L^{1}(SX/G,m_{G})
we can conclude that the geodesic flow is ergodic.

When
X
is Gromov hyperbolic or CAT(0) this convergence property does not holds anymore.
This is one reason why Kaimanovich
[
15
, Theorem 2.6]
and Link
[
17
, Theorem A]
require an additional assumption.
Kaimanovich asks that any two points in the boundary are joined by a unique geodesic, while Link assumes that the space
X
contains a zero-width geodesic (i.e. a geodesic not bounding a flat strip).
Our statement does not make any such hypothesis.
To bypass this difficulty we extend the approach used by Bader-Furman
[
2
]
for Gromov hyperbolic spaces.
See also Coulon-Dougall-Schapira-Tapie
[
10
]
.
The idea is to run the Hopf argument on a different dense subsets of functions which are well behaved for another measurement of the convergence.
If
X
is Gromov hyperbolic, the convergence property can roughly be stated as follows.
Consider two bi-infinite geodesics
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
starting at
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\partial X
and ending both at
\xi\in\partial X
.
As
t
tends to infinity, the visual metric between
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
seen from
\gamma_{i}(t)
decays exponentially.

In our context, there is probably no hope to build a similar global visual metric on
\partial X
.
(Recall that if
G
is a non-trivial free product, then the action on its Cayley graph
X
admits a contracting element.
The space
X
can therefore be pretty uncontrolled.)
However we know that the measure
\nu_{o}
on
\partial X
gives full measure to the radial limit set.
An important part of the article consists in defining a “visual metric” on
\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim
, where
\Lambda
is a suitable recurrent subset of the radial limit set (see
Section 5
).
This allows us to run the Hopf argument with functions built from elements in
\mathcal{E}\otimes L^{1}(\mathbf{R},ds)
, where
\mathcal{E}\subset L^{1}(\Lambda\times\Lambda,\mathfrak{B}^{+}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{+},\mu)
consists of functions which are roughly speaking locally constant for the visual metric (see
Section 6
).

semester in Spring 2023.

2
Groups with a contracting element

Notations and vocabulary.

In this article
(X,d)
is always a proper geodesic metric space.
The open ball of radius
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
centered at
x\in X
is denoted by
B(x,r)
.
Let
Y
be a closed subset of
X
.
Given
x\in X
, a point
y\in Y
, is a
projection
of
x
on
Y
if
\mathchoice{d\left(x,y\right)}{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}=d(x,Y)
.
The
projection
of a subset
Z\subset X
onto
Y
is

\pi_{Y}(Z)=\mathchoice{\left\{y\in Y\;:\ y\ \text{is the projection of a point}\ z\in Z\right\}}{\{y\in Y\;:\ y\ \text{is the projection of a point}\ z\in Z\}}{\{y\in Y\;:\ y\ \text{is the projection of a point}\ z\in Z\}}{\{y\in Y\;:\ y\ \text{is the projection of a point}\ z\in Z\}}.

Let
\gamma\colon I\to X
be a continuous path intersecting
Y
.
The
entry point
and
exit point
of
\gamma
in
Y
are
\gamma(t)
and
\gamma(t^{\prime})
where

t=\inf\mathchoice{\left\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\right\}}{\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\}}{\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\}}{\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\}}\quad\text{and}\quad t^{\prime}=\sup\mathchoice{\left\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\right\}}{\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\}}{\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\}}{\{s\in I\;:\ \gamma(s)\in Y\}}.

If
I
is a compact interval, such points always exist (the subset
Y
is closed).
Given
d\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we denote by
\mathcal{N}_{d}(Y)
the
d
-neighborhood
of
Y
, that is the set of points
x\in X
such that
d(x,Y)\leqslant d
.
The distance between two subsets
Y,Y^{\prime}
of
X
is

d(Y,Y^{\prime})=\inf_{(y,y^{\prime})\in Y\times Y^{\prime}}\mathchoice{d\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}.

We adopt the convention that the diameter of the empty set is zero.

Contracting set.

Definition 2.1

(Contracting set)
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
Y\subset X
be a closed subset.
We say that
Y
is
\alpha
-contracting
if for any geodesic
\gamma
with
d(\gamma,Y)\geqslant\alpha
, we have
\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{Y}(\gamma)\right)}{(\pi_{Y}(\gamma))}{(\pi_{Y}(\gamma))}{(\pi_{Y}(\gamma))}\leqslant\alpha
.
The set
Y
is
contracting
, if
Y
is
\alpha
-contracting for some
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}^{*}_{+}
.

The next statements are direct consequences of the definition.
Their proofs are left to the reader.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
Y
be an
\alpha
-contracting subset.
If
\gamma
is a geodesic joining two points of
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(Y)
, then
\gamma
lies in the
(5\alpha/2)
-neighborhood of
Y
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
Y
be an
\alpha
-contracting subset.
Let
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
\gamma
be a geodesic from
x
to
x^{\prime}
.
Let
p
and
p^{\prime}
be respective projections of
x
and
x^{\prime}
onto
Y
.
If
d(x,Y)<\alpha
or
\mathchoice{d\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}>\alpha
, then the following holds:

(i)

d(\gamma,Y)<\alpha
;

(ii)

the entry point ( respectively exit point) of
\gamma
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(Y)
is
2\alpha
-closed to
p
( respectively
p^{\prime}
);

(iii)

\mathchoice{d\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,p\right)}{d(x,p)}{d(x,p)}{d(x,p)}+\mathchoice{d\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},x^{\prime}\right)}{d(p^{\prime},x^{\prime})}{d(p^{\prime},x^{\prime})}{d(p^{\prime},x^{\prime})}-8\alpha
.

Remark 2.1
.

The last inequality can be improved if
x
or
x^{\prime}
already belongs to
Y
.
It follows from the above statement that the nearest-point projection onto
Y
is large-scale
1
-Lipschitz.
More precisely, if
Z\subset X
is a subset then
\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{Y}(Z)\right)}{(\pi_{Y}(Z))}{(\pi_{Y}(Z))}{(\pi_{Y}(Z))}\leqslant\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(Z\right)}{(Z)}{(Z)}{(Z)}+4\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
Y,Y^{\prime}\subset X
be two
\alpha
-contracting sets.
For every
A\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y^{\prime})\right)}{(\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{A}(Y^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime})\right)}{(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime}))}+2A+4\alpha,

\displaystyle\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime})\right)}{(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y)\cap\mathcal{N}_{5\alpha/2}(Y^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{Y}(Y^{\prime})\right)}{(\pi_{Y}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\pi_{Y}(Y^{\prime}))}{(\pi_{Y}(Y^{\prime}))}+14\alpha.

Contracting element.

Let
o\in X
be a base point.
Consider a group
G
acting properly by isometries on
X
.
For every
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
we denote by
B_{G}(r)
the ball of radius
r
in
G
for the pseudo-metric induced by
X
, that is

B_{G}(r)=\mathchoice{\left\{g\in G\;:\ \mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\leqslant r\right\}}{\{g\in G\;:\ \mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\leqslant r\}}{\{g\in G\;:\ \mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\leqslant r\}}{\{g\in G\;:\ \mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\leqslant r\}}.

(1)

Similarly, given
r,a\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, the induced sphere of radius
r
in
G
is

S_{G}(r,a)=\mathchoice{\left\{g\in G\;:\ r-a\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}<r+a\right\}}{\{g\in G\;:\ r-a\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}<r+a\}}{\{g\in G\;:\ r-a\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}<r+a\}}{\{g\in G\;:\ r-a\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}<r+a\}}.

(2)

Definition 2.5

(Contracting element)
.

An element
g\in G
is
contracting
, for its action on
X
, if the orbit map
\mathbf{Z}\to X
sending
n
to
g^{n}x
is a quasi-isometric embedding with contracting image.

Let
g\in G
be a contracting element.
We call
axis
of
g
any closed subset
Y\subset X
such that the Hausdorff distance between
Y
and some (hence any) orbit of
\left\langle g\right\rangle
is finite.
By definition it is quasi-isometric to
\mathbf{R}
.
Define
E(g)
as the set of elements
u\in G
such that the Hausdorff distance between
Y
and
uY
is finite.
It follows from the definition that
E(g)
is a subgroup of
G
that does not depend on the axis
Y
.
It is the maximal, virtually cyclic subgroup of
G
containing
\left\langle g\right\rangle
.
Moreover,
E(g)
is almost-malnormal, that is
uE(g)u^{-1}\cap E(g)
is finite, for every
u\in G\setminus E(g)
.
There is
C\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that for every
u\in G
,

—

\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{Y}(uY)\right)}{(\pi_{Y}(uY))}{(\pi_{Y}(uY))}{(\pi_{Y}(uY))}\leqslant C
, if
u\notin E(g)
;

—

the Hausdorff distance between
Y
and
uY
is at most
C
, if
u\in E(g)
;

(of course the parameter
C
does depend on the chosen axis
Y
).
See Yang
[
29
, Lemma 2.11]
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
g\in G
be a contracting element and
Y
an axis of
g
.
There is
D\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with the following property: if
H\subset G
is a subgroup that does not contain a contracting element, then the projection on
Y
of

\bigcup_{h\in H\setminus E(g)}hY

has diameter at most
D
.

Proof.

Since the statement is of qualitative nature, we only sketch the proof.
In particular, all our estimates will be very generous.
We refer the reader to Arzhantseva-Cashen-Tao
[
1
, section 3]
or Yang
[
29
, Section 2.3]
where similar arguments are run with precise computations.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
Y
is a bi-infinite geodesic
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
.
We fix
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that
\gamma
is
\alpha
-contracting and
\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{\gamma}(u\gamma)\right)}{(\pi_{\gamma}(u\gamma))}{(\pi_{\gamma}(u\gamma))}{(\pi_{\gamma}(u\gamma))}\leqslant\alpha
, for every
u\in G\setminus E(g)
.
We begin with the following claim.

Claim 2.7
.

There is
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that for every
h\in G\setminus E(g)
, if

d\left(\pi_{\gamma}(h^{-1}\gamma),\pi_{\gamma}(h\gamma)\right)>L,

then
h
is contracting.

Up to changing the parametrization of
\gamma
we can assume that the projection on
\gamma
of
h^{-1}\gamma
and
h\gamma
are respectively contained in
\gamma(I_{-})
and
\gamma(I_{+})
where
I_{-}=\mathchoice{\left[-\alpha\,,0\right]}{[-\alpha\,,0]}{[-\alpha\,,0]}{[-\alpha\,,0]}
and
I_{+}=\mathchoice{\left[\ell\,,\ell+\alpha\right]}{[\ell\,,\ell+\alpha]}{[\ell\,,\ell+\alpha]}{[\ell\,,\ell+\alpha]}
for some
\ell\in[L,\infty)
.
Let
m=\gamma(\ell/2)
.
By construction any projection of
h^{-1}m
( respectively
hm
) on
\gamma
belongs to
\gamma(I_{-})
( respectively
\gamma(I_{+})
).
Hence if
L
is sufficiently large then
m
is
5\alpha
-close to any geodesic from
h^{-1}m
to
hm
.
Denote by
\nu\colon\mathchoice{\left[0\,,T\right]}{[0\,,T]}{[0\,,T]}{[0\,,T]}\to X
a geodesic from
h^{-1}m
to
m
.
Note that the concatenation of
\nu
and
h\nu
is “almost” a geodesic from
h^{-1}m
to
hm
(see
Figure 1
).
We extend
\nu
to a bi-infinite
\left\langle h\right\rangle
-invariant path, still denoted by
\nu\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
, which is characterized as follows:
\nu(t+kT)=h^{k}\nu(t)
for every
t\in\mathbf{R}
and
k\in\mathbf{Z}
.
One proves that
\nu
is a quasi-geodesic which fellow-travels for a long time with
h^{k}\gamma
, for every
k\in\mathbf{Z}
.
This suffices to show that
h
is contracting and completes the proof of our claim.

We now fix a set
S\subset H
of representatives of
H/H\cap E(g)
.
We assume that the representative of
H\cap E(g)
is the trivial element.
Recall that the Hausdorff between
\gamma
and
u\gamma
is uniformly bounded as
u
runs over
E(g)
.
Hence it suffices to bound the diameter of the projection on
\gamma
of the set

Z=\bigcup_{h\in S\setminus\{1\}}h\gamma

Let
h_{1},h_{2}\in S\setminus\{1\}
be two distinct elements.
Without loss of generality we can assume that

d\left(\pi_{\gamma}(h_{1}\gamma),\pi_{\gamma}(h_{2}\gamma)\right)\geqslant 100\alpha.

Set
h=h_{2}^{-1}h_{1}
.
Note that
\mathchoice{d\left(q_{1},q_{2}\right)}{d(q_{1},q_{2})}{d(q_{1},q_{2})}{d(q_{1},q_{2})}\geqslant 100\alpha
.
By construction,

d\left(\pi_{h_{1}^{-1}\gamma}\left(\gamma\right),\pi_{h_{1}^{-1}\gamma}\left(h^{-1}\gamma\right)\right)\geqslant d\left(\pi_{\gamma}\left(h_{1}\gamma\right),\pi_{\gamma}\left(h_{2}\gamma\right)\right)\geqslant 100\alpha

One shows using
Lemma 2.3
that the projection of
h^{-1}\gamma
on
\gamma
lies in the
10\alpha
-neihgbourhood of
\pi_{\gamma}(h_{1}^{-1}\gamma)
(see
Figure 2
).

Similarly one observes that

d\left(\pi_{h_{2}^{-1}\gamma}\left(\gamma\right),\pi_{h_{2}^{-1}\gamma}\left(h\gamma\right)\right)\geqslant d\left(\pi_{\gamma}\left(h_{2}\gamma\right),\pi_{\gamma}\left(h_{1}\gamma\right)\right)\geqslant 100\alpha

and proves that the projection of
h\gamma
on
\gamma
lies in the
10\alpha
-neihgbourhood of
\pi_{\gamma}(h_{2}^{-1}\gamma)
.
In view of our previous claim

d\left(\pi_{\gamma}(h_{i}^{-1}\gamma),\pi_{\gamma}(h_{i}\gamma)\right)\leqslant L,\quad\forall i\in\{1,2\}.

Consequently

\displaystyle d\left(\pi_{\gamma}(h^{-1}\gamma),\pi_{\gamma}(h\gamma)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant d\left(\pi_{\gamma}(h_{1}^{-1}\gamma),\pi_{\gamma}(h_{2}^{-1}\gamma)\right)-50\alpha

\displaystyle\geqslant d\left(\pi_{\gamma}(h_{1}\gamma),\pi_{\gamma}(h_{2}\gamma)\right)-2L-100\alpha.

Since
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are distinct,
h
is neither contracting nor belongs to
E(g)
.
It follows from our previous claim that the projection of
Z
on
\gamma
has diameter at most
3L+200\alpha
.
∎

3
Compactification of
X

3.1
Horocompactification

Let
C(X)
be the set of all continuous functions on
X
endowed with the topology of uniform convergence on every compact subset.
We denote by
C^{*}(X)
the quotient of
C(X)
by the subspace consisting of all constant functions.
It is endowed with the quotient topology.
Alternatively
C^{*}(X)
is the set of continuous cocycles
c\colon X\times X\to\mathbf{R}
.
By
cocycle
we mean that
c(x,z)=c(x,y)+c(y,z)
, for every
x,y,z\in X
.
For example, given
z\in X
, we define the cocycle
b_{z}\colon X\times X\to\mathbf{R}
by

b_{z}(x,y)=\mathchoice{d\left(x,z\right)}{d(x,z)}{d(x,z)}{d(x,z)}-\mathchoice{d\left(y,z\right)}{d(y,z)}{d(y,z)}{d(y,z)},\quad\forall x,y\in X.

Since
X
is geodesic, the map

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\iota\colon&X&\to&C^{*}(X)\\
&z&\mapsto&b_{z}\end{array}

is a homeomorphism from
X
onto its image.

Definition 3.1

(Horoboundary)
.

The
horocompactification

\bar{X}
of
X
it the closure of
\iota(X)
in
C^{*}(X)
.
The
horoboundary
of
X
is the set
\partial X=\bar{X}\setminus\iota(X)
.

From now on, we often identify
X
and its image in
C^{*}(X)
.

Example 3.1
.

Any geodesic ray
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X
defines a
Busemann cocycle

c^{\prime}_{\gamma}\in\partial X
given by

c^{\prime}_{\gamma}(x,y)=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mathchoice{d\left(x,\gamma(t)\right)}{d(x,\gamma(t))}{d(x,\gamma(t))}{d(x,\gamma(t))}-\mathchoice{d\left(y,\gamma(t)\right)}{d(y,\gamma(t))}{d(y,\gamma(t))}{d(y,\gamma(t))},\quad\forall x,y\in X.

If
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
is a bi-infinite geodesic, we also denote by
c_{\gamma}
the Busemann cocycle associated to the reverse path
\bar{\gamma}
, that is such that
\bar{\gamma}(t)=\gamma(-t)
, for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
.
We think of
\gamma
as a bi-infinite geodesic going from
c_{\gamma}
to
c^{\prime}_{\gamma}
.
∎

By construction, every cocycle
c\in\bar{X}
is
1
-Lipschitz, or equivalently
\mathchoice{\left|c(x,x^{\prime})\right|}{|c(x,x^{\prime})|}{|c(x,x^{\prime})|}{|c(x,x^{\prime})|}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
.
Recall that
X
is proper.
It is a consequence of the Azerla-Ascoli theorem, that the horocompactification
\bar{X}
is indeed a compact set.
We denote by
\mathfrak{B}
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\bar{X}
.
Any isometry
g
of
X
extends to a homeomorphism of
\bar{X}
as follows: for every
c\in\bar{X}
, the cocycle
gc
is defined by

gc(x,y)=c(g^{-1}x,g^{-1}y),\quad\forall x,y\in X.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
c\in\bar{X}
.
A
gradient arc
for
c
is a path
\nu\colon I\to X
parametrized by arc length such that
c(\nu(s),\nu(t))=t-s
, for every
s,t\in I
.
If
I
has the form
[a,\infty)
or
I=\mathbf{R}
, we use the word
gradient ray
and
gradient line
respectively.

Given a gradient arc
\nu\colon I\to X
for
c
, we say that
\nu

ends at
c
if

—

either
c=\iota(z)
for some
z\in X
and
\nu
ends at
z
,

—

or
c\in\partial X
and
I
is not bounded from above.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
.
A
complete gradient arc
from
c
to
c^{\prime}
is a gradient arc
\nu\colon I\to X
for
c^{\prime}
, ending at
c^{\prime}
such that the reverse path
\bar{\nu}
given by
\bar{\nu}(t)=\nu(-t)
is a gradient arc for
c
, ending at
c
.

If both
c
and
c^{\prime}
belong to
X
, then any complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
is just a geodesic from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.
If
c
or
c^{\prime}
belongs to
X
, a complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
always exists, see
[
9
, Lemma 3.4]
.
It follows that
\iota(X)
is open in
\bar{X}
.
We will discuss later the existence of complete gradient arcs joining two point in the horoboundary, see
Lemma 3.5
.

3.2
Reduced horoboundary

Given a cocycle
c\in C^{*}(X)
we write
\mathchoice{\left\|c\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}
for its uniform norm, i.e.

\mathchoice{\left\|c\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}=\sup_{x,x^{\prime}\in X}\mathchoice{\left|c(x,x^{\prime})\right|}{|c(x,x^{\prime})|}{|c(x,x^{\prime})|}{|c(x,x^{\prime})|}.

Similarly, if
K\subset X
is compact, then
\mathchoice{\left\|c\right\|_{K}}{\|c\|_{K}}{\|c\|_{K}}{\|c\|_{K}}
is the uniform norm of
c
restricted to
K\times K
.
Note that
\mathchoice{\left\|c\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}{\|c\|_{\infty}}
can be infinite.
We endow
\bar{X}
with a binary relation: two cocycles
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
are
equivalent
, and we write
c\sim c^{\prime}
, if one of the following holds:

—

either
c,c^{\prime}
lie in the image of
\iota\colon X\to C^{*}(X)
and
c=c^{\prime}
,

—

or
c,c^{\prime}\in\partial X
and
\mathchoice{\left\|c-c^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}<\infty
.

If
c\in\bar{X}
, we write
[c]
for its equivalence class.

Given a subset
B\subset\bar{X}
, the
saturation
of
B
, denoted by
B^{+}
, is the union of all equivalence classes intersecting
B
.
We say that
B
is
saturated
if
B^{+}=B
.
Note that the collection of saturated subsets is closed under complement and (uncountable) union and intersection.
The
reduced algebra
, denoted by
\mathfrak{B}^{+}
, is the sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathfrak{B}
which consists of all saturated Borel subsets.
The saturation of any closed set belongs to
\mathfrak{B}^{+}
, see
[
9
, Lemma 3.7]
.

Remark 3.1
.

In this article we often make the following abuse of notations:
If
(\Omega,\mathfrak{A})
is a measurable space, and
\Omega_{0}\in\mathfrak{A}
a subset of
\Omega
, we write
(\Omega_{0},\mathfrak{A})
for the measurable space
\Omega_{0}
endowed with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{0}=\mathchoice{\left\{A\cap\Omega_{0}\;:\ A\in\mathfrak{A}\right\}}{\{A\cap\Omega_{0}\;:\ A\in\mathfrak{A}\}}{\{A\cap\Omega_{0}\;:\ A\in\mathfrak{A}\}}{\{A\cap\Omega_{0}\;:\ A\in\mathfrak{A}\}}
.
∎

Definition 3.4
.

The measurable space
(\partial X,\mathfrak{B}^{+})
is called the
reduced horoboundary
of the space
X
.

3.3
Gromov product.

Given
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
, and
x\in X
, we define the following Gromov product

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{z\in X}\left[c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\right],

(3)

taking values in
\mathbf{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
.
The action of
G
on
\bar{X}
preserves Gromov products.
Since cocycles are
1
-Lispchitz, if
c^{\prime}=\iota(y)
for some
y\in X
, then

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,y)+\mathchoice{d\left(x,y\right)}{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}\right].

Moreover
0\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,y\right)}{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}{d(x,y)}
.
In particular, if both
c
and
c^{\prime}
belong to
X
, we recover the usual definition of the Gromov product.
If
c,c^{\prime}\in\partial X
are equivalent, i.e.
\mathchoice{\left\|c-c^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}<\infty
, then
\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}=\infty
.
The converse does not always hold true though.
Note that for every,
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,x^{\prime})+c^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})\right]+\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}.

(4)

In addition, for all
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
, for all
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in X
, we have,

\mathchoice{\left|\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}\right|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}{\langle c^{\prime},y^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}}|}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{\left\|c-c^{\prime}\right\|_{K}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{K}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{K}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{K}}+\mathchoice{d\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}+\mathchoice{d\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})},

(5)

where
K\subset X
is any compact set containing
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
y
, and
y^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\partial X
.
Assume that there is
z\in X
such that
\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}=0
.
Then there exists a complete gradient line from
c
to
c^{\prime}
going through
z
.

Remark 3.2
.

In view of (
4
) the assumption is equivalent to the following fact:
there is
z\in X
, such that for some (hence any)
x\in X
.

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\right].\qed

Proof.

Denote by
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X
( respectively
\gamma^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X
) a gradient line from
x
to
c
( respectively to
c^{\prime}
).
Such lines exist by
[
9
, Lemma 3.4]
.
Build a path
\nu\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
as follows

\begin{cases}\gamma^{\prime}(t)&\text{if}\ t\geqslant 0,\\
\gamma(-t)&\text{if}\ t\leqslant 0.\end{cases}

We claim that
c^{\prime}(\gamma(t),z)=t
, for every
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Since
\gamma
is a gradient line for
c
, we observe that

t+c^{\prime}(z,\gamma(t))\leqslant c(z,\gamma(t))+c^{\prime}(z,\gamma(t))\leqslant 2\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}\leqslant 0.

Hence
c^{\prime}(\gamma(t),z)\geqslant t
.
Recall that
c^{\prime}
is
1
-Lipchitz, thus it forces
c^{\prime}(\gamma(t),z)=t
, as we announced.
Consequently
c^{\prime}(\nu(t),z)=-t
, for every
t\in\mathbf{R}
.
It follows from the cocycle property that
c^{\prime}(\nu(s),\nu(t))=t-s
, for every
s,t\in\mathbf{R}
.
In other words,
\nu
is a gradient line for
c^{\prime}
.
By symmetry we get that the reverse path
t\mapsto\nu(-t)
is also a gradient line for
c
.
∎

The map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bar{X}\times X\times X&\to&\mathbf{R}_{+}\\
(c,y,x)&\mapsto&\mathchoice{\left\langle c,y\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,y\rangle_{x}}\end{array}

is continuous.
However its extension to
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\to\mathbf{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
may not be continuous anymore.
We claim that it is Borel though.
Indeed for every
N\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, the set

A_{N}=\mathchoice{\left\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant N\right\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant N\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant N\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant N\}}.

can be written

A_{N}=\bigcap_{z\in X}\mathchoice{\left\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\leqslant 2N\right\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\leqslant 2N\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\leqslant 2N\}}{\{(c,c^{\prime},x)\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\;:\ c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\leqslant 2N\}}.

As an intersection of closed subsets,
A_{N}
is closed as well, whence our claim.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(c,c^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
.
Assume that there exist a neighborhood
U\subset\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
of
(c,c^{\prime})
and a compact subset
K\subset X
with the following property: for every pair
(y,y^{\prime})\in(X\times X)\cap U
, there is a geodesic from
y
to
y^{\prime}
intersecting
K
.
Then, there is a point
z_{0}\in K
such that the following holds.

(i)

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{z_{0}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z_{0}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z_{0}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z_{0}}}=0
.

(ii)

There is a complete gradient line from
c
to
c^{\prime}
going through
z_{0}
.

(iii)

For every
x\in X
, the map
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\times X\to\mathbf{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
sending
(b,b^{\prime},z)
to
\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{z}}
is continuous at
(c,c^{\prime},x)
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in X
.
For simplicity we let

\ell=\sup_{z\in K}\ \frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,z)+c^{\prime}(x,z)\right].

The set
K
is compact, while
c
and
c^{\prime}
are continuous.
Hence the upper bound
\ell
is achieved at some point
z_{0}\in K
.
Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Recall that
\bar{X}
is endowed with the topology of uniform convergence on every compact subset.
There is an open set
V\subset U
of
(c,c^{\prime})
such that for every
(b,b^{\prime})\in V
, the cocycle
b
( respectively
b^{\prime}
) restricted to
\{x\}\times K
differs from
c
( respectively
c^{\prime}
) by at most
\varepsilon
.

Let
z\in X
and set
K_{0}=K\cup\{z\}
.
Let
(b,b^{\prime})\in V
.
Since
V
is open, there are
(y,y^{\prime})\in(X\times X)\cap V
whose corresponding cocycles
\tilde{b}
and
\tilde{b}^{\prime}
, when restricted to
\{x\}\times K_{0}
, differ from
b
and
b^{\prime}
respectively by at most
\varepsilon
.
By construction
(y,y^{\prime})\in U
.
Thus there is a point
p\in K
on a geodesic from
y
to
y^{\prime}
.
Using the triangle inequality we observe that

\frac{1}{2}\left[\tilde{b}(x,z)+\tilde{b}^{\prime}(x,z)\right]\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle y,y^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[\tilde{b}(x,p)+\tilde{b}^{\prime}(x,p)\right].

According to our choices,
b
and
\tilde{b}
differs by at most
\varepsilon
on
(x,z)
while
c
and
\tilde{b}
differs by at most
\varepsilon
on
(x,p)
.
A similar statement holds for
b^{\prime}
,
\tilde{b}^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
.
Consequenlty, the previous inequality yields

\frac{1}{2}\left[b(x,z)+b^{\prime}(x,z)\right]-\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,p)+c^{\prime}(x,p)\right]+\varepsilon

Consequently

\frac{1}{2}\left[b(x,z)+b^{\prime}(x,z)\right]\leqslant\ell\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}+2\varepsilon.

This inequality holds for every
z\in X
so that

\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant\ell\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}+2\varepsilon.

Note that
\ell
does not depend on the choice of
b
and
b^{\prime}
, thus
(b,b^{\prime})\mapsto\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}
is uniformly bounded on
V
.
The previous inequality shows in particular that,

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}=\ell=\frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,z_{0})+c^{\prime}(x,z_{0})\right],

which proves
(i)
.
As we noticed in
Remark 3.2
, it means in particular that
\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{z_{0}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z_{0}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z_{0}}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{z_{0}}}=0
.
Thus there is complete gradient line from
c
to
c^{\prime}
(
Lemma 3.5
) which completes the proof of
(ii)
.

We are left to prove
(iii)
.
We use the same notations as above.
Recall that
b
and
c
( respectively
b^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
) differ by at most
\varepsilon
on
\{x\}\times K
.
Hence

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[c(x,z_{0})+c^{\prime}(x,z_{0})\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[b(x,z_{0})+b^{\prime}(x,z_{0})\right]+\varepsilon\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}+\varepsilon.

To summarize we have proved that for every
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
there is a neighborhood
V
of
(c,c^{\prime})
, such that for every
(b,b^{\prime})\in V
, we have

\mathchoice{\left|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\right|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}|}\leqslant 2\varepsilon.

Recall that cocycles in
\bar{X}
are
1
-Lipschitz.
Using (
4
) we can vary the base point.
More precisely for every
y\in B(x,\varepsilon)
, the previous inequality yields

\mathchoice{\left|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}\right|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}|}{|\mathchoice{\left\langle b,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}-\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{x}}|}\leqslant 3\varepsilon,

whence the result.
∎

3.4
Extended boundary of a contracting set

Definition 3.7
.

Let
Y
be a closed subset of
X
.
Let
c\in\bar{X}
.
A
projection
of
c
on
Y
is a point
q\in Y
such that for every
y\in Y
, we have
c(q,y)\leqslant 0
.

Given
z\in X
, the projection of
b=\iota(z)
on
Y
coincides with the definition of the nearest-point projection of
z
.
If
c
belongs to
\partial X
, a projection of
c
on
Y
may exist or not.
This leads to the following definition.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
Y
be a closed subset of
X
.
The
extended boundary
of
Y
, denoted by
\partial^{+}Y
is the set of all cocycles
c\in\partial X
for which there is no projection of
c
on
Y
.

If
Y
is contracting, then
\partial^{+}Y
is saturated (as the notation suggests)
[
9
, Proposition 3.9]
.
The next statements extend
Lemma 2.3
for a gradient ray joining two points in
\bar{X}
.

Lemma 3.9

(Coulon
[
9
, Corollary 3.13]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Let
Y
an
\alpha
-contracting set.
Let
x\in X
and
c\in\bar{X}\setminus\partial^{+}Y
.
Let
p
and
q
be respective projections of
x
and
c
on
Y
.
Let
\nu
be a complete gradient arc from
x
to
c
.
If
d(x,Y)<\alpha
or
\mathchoice{d\left(p,q\right)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}>4\alpha
, then the following holds

—

d(\nu,Y)<\alpha
;

—

the entry point ( respectively exit point) of
\nu
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(Y)
is
2\alpha
-closed ( respectively
5\alpha
-closed) to
p
( respectively
q
);

—

\mathchoice{d\left(x,p\right)}{d(x,p)}{d(x,p)}{d(x,p)}+\mathchoice{d\left(p,q\right)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}-14\alpha\leqslant c(x,q)\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,p\right)}{d(x,p)}{d(x,p)}{d(x,p)}+\mathchoice{d\left(p,q\right)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
.
Let
Y
an
\alpha
-contracting set and
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}\setminus\partial^{+}Y
.
Let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be respective projections of
c
and
c^{\prime}
on
Y
.
If
\mathchoice{d\left(q,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}>7\alpha
, then the following holds.

(i)

There is a complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.

(ii)

For any complete gradient arc
\nu\colon I\to X
from
c
to
c^{\prime}
, we have
d(\nu,\gamma)<\alpha
.
Moreover the entry point ( respectively exit point) of
\nu
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(\gamma)
is
5\alpha
-close to
q
( respectively
q^{\prime}
).

Proof.

We start with the existence of a complete gradient line.
Denote by
K
the closed ball of radius
4\alpha
centered at
q
.
In view of
Proposition 3.6
it suffices to prove that there is an neighborhood
U
of
(c,c^{\prime})
in
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
such that for every
(z,z^{\prime})\in(X\times X)\cap U
, any geodesic from
z
to
z^{\prime}
intersects
K
.
Assume on the contrary that this fact does not hold.
We can find two sequences
(z_{n})
and
(z^{\prime}_{n})
of points in
X
converging respectively to
c
and
c^{\prime}
and a geodesic from
z_{n}
to
z^{\prime}_{n}
that does not cross
K
.
Denote by
q_{n}
and
q^{\prime}_{n}
respective projections of
z_{n}
and
z^{\prime}_{n}
on
Y
.
Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
(q_{n})
and
(q^{\prime}_{n})
respectively converges to
q_{*},q^{\prime}_{*}\in Y
which are also respective projections of
c
and
c^{\prime}
on
Y
, see
[
9
, Proposition 3.11]
.
According to
[
9
, Corollary 3.12]
we have
\mathchoice{d\left(q,q^{*}\right)}{d(q,q^{*})}{d(q,q^{*})}{d(q,q^{*})}\leqslant\alpha
and
\mathchoice{d\left(q^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{*}\right)}{d(q^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{*})}{d(q^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{*})}{d(q^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{*})}\leqslant\alpha
.
Consequently
\mathchoice{d\left(q_{n},q^{\prime}_{n}\right)}{d(q_{n},q^{\prime}_{n})}{d(q_{n},q^{\prime}_{n})}{d(q_{n},q^{\prime}_{n})}>\alpha
, provided
n
is sufficiently large.
It follows from
Lemma 2.3
that
q_{n}
(and
q^{\prime}_{n}
) are
2\alpha
-close to any geodesic from
z_{n}
to
z^{\prime}_{n}
.
Since
\mathchoice{d\left(q,q_{*}\right)}{d(q,q_{*})}{d(q,q_{*})}{d(q,q_{*})}\leqslant\alpha
, this contradicts our assumption, provided
n
is sufficiently large.

We now prove
(ii)
.
Let
\nu\colon I\to X
be an arbitrary complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.
Let

T=\inf\mathchoice{\left\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\right\}}{\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\}}{\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\}}{\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\}}\quad\text{and}\quad T^{\prime}=\sup\mathchoice{\left\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\right\}}{\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\}}{\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\}}{\{t\in I\;:\ d(\nu(t),Y)\leqslant\alpha\}}

with the convention that
T=\sup I
and
T^{\prime}=\inf I
, whenever
\nu
does not intersect
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(\gamma)
.
Set

Q=\pi_{Y}\left(\mathchoice{\left\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\leqslant T\right\}}{\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\leqslant T\}}{\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\leqslant T\}}{\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\leqslant T\}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad Q^{\prime}=\pi_{Y}\left(\mathchoice{\left\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\geqslant T^{\prime}\right\}}{\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\geqslant T^{\prime}\}}{\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\geqslant T^{\prime}\}}{\{\nu(t)\;:\ t\geqslant T^{\prime}\}}\right)

According to
[
9
, Proposition 3.11]
, the point
q_{*}
( respectively
q^{\prime}_{*}
) lies in the
\alpha
-neighborhood of
Q
( respectively
Q^{\prime}
) while
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
have diameter at most
\alpha
.
It follows from the triangle inequality combined with our assumption that

d(Q,Q^{\prime})\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(q_{*},q^{\prime}_{*}\right)}{d(q_{*},q^{\prime}_{*})}{d(q_{*},q^{\prime}_{*})}{d(q_{*},q^{\prime}_{*})}-4\alpha\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(q,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}-6\alpha>\alpha.

Since
\gamma
is contracting,
d(\nu,Y)<\alpha
.
In particular,
\nu(T)
and
\nu(T^{\prime})
are the entry and exit point of
\nu
in
Y
.
Moreover
d(\nu(T),q)\leqslant 5\alpha
and
d(\nu(T^{\prime}),q^{\prime})\leqslant 5\alpha
, which completes the proof of
(ii)
.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}^{*}_{+}
.
Let
Y
be an
\alpha
-contracting set.
Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}\setminus\partial^{+}Y
.
Let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be respective projections of
c
and
c^{\prime}
on
Y
.
If
\mathchoice{\left\|c-c^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}<\infty
, then
\mathchoice{d\left(q,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}\leqslant 4\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
\nu\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X
be gradient line for
c
starting at
q
.
Note that
q
is a projection of
\nu(t)
on
Y
for any
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}

[
9
, Lemma 3.9]
.
Assume now that contrary to our claim
\mathchoice{d\left(q,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}>4\alpha
.
Let
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
It follows from
Lemma 3.9
applied with
x=\nu(t)
and
c^{\prime}
that

c^{\prime}(\nu(t),q)\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(q,\nu(t)\right)}{d(q,\nu(t))}{d(q,\nu(t))}{d(q,\nu(t))}-4\alpha\geqslant t-4\alpha.

However since
\nu
is a gradient line for
c
, we also have
c(\nu(t),q)=-t
.
These two estimates hold for every
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and contradict the fact that
\mathchoice{\left\|c-c^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}<\infty
.
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
g\in G
be a contracting element and
Y
an axis of
g
.
Let
c\in\bar{X}
.
Let
H\subset G
be a subgroup that does not contain a contracting element.
Assume that the intersection
Hc\cap\partial^{+}Y
is empty.
Then the projection on
Y
of
Hc
is bounded.

Proof.

We fix
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that
Y
is
\alpha
-contracting and
\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{Y}(uY)\right)}{(\pi_{Y}(uY))}{(\pi_{Y}(uY))}{(\pi_{Y}(uY))}\leqslant\alpha
, for every
u\in G\setminus E(g)
.
Denote by
Z
the set

Z=\bigcup_{h\in H\setminus E(g)}hY.

According to
Proposition 2.6
the projection of
Z
on
Y
has bounded diameter.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that there is
h_{0}\in H
such that
d(\pi_{Y}(h_{0}c),\pi_{Y}(Z))>4\alpha
.
Up to replacing
c
by a translate of
c
, we can also assume that
h_{0}=1
.
Let
h\in H\setminus E(g)
.
According to our previous choices

d(\pi_{hY}(hc),\pi_{hY}(Y))\geqslant d(\pi_{Y}(c),\pi_{Y}(h^{-1}Y))\geqslant d(\pi_{Y}(c),\pi_{Y}(Z))>4\alpha.

One proves using
Lemma 3.9
that any projection of
hc
on
Y
lies in the
15\alpha
-neighborhood of
\pi_{Y}(hY)
hence of
\pi_{Y}(Z)
, see
Figure 3
.

\endlabellist
