Projections of
c
and
hc
on
Y

Since the later set is bounded, we get that the projection on
Y
of
(H\setminus E(g))c
is bounded.
Recall that
H
does not contain any contracting element.
Consequently
H\cap E(g)
is finite.
Therefore the projection of
Hc
on
Y
has bounded diameter at well.
∎

3.5
Shadows

We now recall the definition of shadows which will be an essential tool to study conformal measures supported on the horocompactification.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
x,y\in X
.
Let
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
The
r
-shadow
of
y
seen from
x
, is the set

\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r)=\mathchoice{\left\{c\in\bar{X}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}\leqslant r\right\}}{\{c\in\bar{X}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}\leqslant r\}}{\{c\in\bar{X}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}\leqslant r\}}{\{c\in\bar{X}\;:\ \mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{y}}\leqslant r\}}.

By construction,
\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r)
is a closed subset of
\bar{X}
.
It follows from (
5
) that for every
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in X
and
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
,

\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{x^{\prime}}(y^{\prime},r^{\prime}),\quad\text{where}\quad r^{\prime}=r+\mathchoice{d\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}{d(x,x^{\prime})}+\mathchoice{d\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}.

(6)

Definition 3.14
.

Let
x,y\in X
.
Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
We say that the pair
(x,y)
has an
(\alpha,L)
-contracting tail
if there is an
\alpha
-contracting geodesic
\gamma
ending at
y
and a projection
p
of
x
on
\gamma
satisfying
\mathchoice{d\left(p,y\right)}{d(p,y)}{d(p,y)}{d(p,y)}\geqslant L
.
The path
\gamma
is called a
tail
of
(x,y)
(the parameters
\alpha
and
L
should be clear from the context).

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>r+13\alpha
.
Let
x,y\in X
.
Assume
(x,y)
has an
(\alpha,L)
-contracting tail, say
\gamma
.
Let
p
be a projection of
x
on
\gamma
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r)
.
Let
q
be a projection of
c
onto
\gamma
. Then the following holds.

(i)

\mathchoice{d\left(y,q\right)}{d(y,q)}{d(y,q)}{d(y,q)}\leqslant r+7\alpha
.

(ii)

For any complete gradient arc
\nu\colon I\to X
from
x
to
c
, we have
d(\nu,\gamma)<\alpha
.
Moreover the entry point ( respectively exit point) of
\nu
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(\gamma)
is
2\alpha
-close to
p
( respectively
5\alpha
-close to
q
).

(iii)

\mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}\leqslant 4\alpha
, for every point
z
on
\gamma
with
r+7\alpha\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(z,y\right)}{d(z,y)}{d(z,y)}{d(z,y)}\leqslant L
.

(iv)

\mathchoice{\left\langle z^{\prime},c\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle z^{\prime},c\rangle_{z}}{\langle z^{\prime},c\rangle_{z}}{\langle z^{\prime},c\rangle_{z}}\leqslant 2\alpha
, for every points
z,z^{\prime}
on
\gamma
with
r+7\alpha\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(z,y\right)}{d(z,y)}{d(z,y)}{d(z,y)}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(z^{\prime},y\right)}{d(z^{\prime},y)}{d(z^{\prime},y)}{d(z^{\prime},y)}
.

Proof.

The first two points correspond to
[
9
, Lemma 4.14]
.
By definition of contracting tail, there is a projection
p^{\prime}
of
x
on
\gamma
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},y\right)}{d(p^{\prime},y)}{d(p^{\prime},y)}{d(p^{\prime},y)}\geqslant L
.
Let
(b_{n})
be a sequence of cocycles in
\iota(X)
converging to
c
.
For every
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we write
q_{n}
for a projection of
b_{n}
on
\gamma
.
Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
(q_{n})
converges to a point
q^{*}
which is a projection of
c
on
\gamma

[
9
, Lemma 3.11]
.
Hence
\mathchoice{d\left(y,q_{*}\right)}{d(y,q_{*})}{d(y,q_{*})}{d(y,q_{*})}\leqslant r+7\alpha
.
Consider now a point
z
on
\gamma
such that
r+7\alpha<\mathchoice{d\left(z,y\right)}{d(z,y)}{d(z,y)}{d(z,y)}\leqslant L
.
For all but finitely many
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
z
lies on
\gamma
between
p^{\prime}
and
q_{n}
, while
\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},q_{n}\right)}{d(p^{\prime},q_{n})}{d(p^{\prime},q_{n})}{d(p^{\prime},q_{n})}>\alpha
.
Hence
\mathchoice{\left\langle x,b_{n}\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,b_{n}\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,b_{n}\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,b_{n}\rangle_{z}}\leqslant 4\alpha
(
Lemma 2.3
).
Passing to the limit, we get
\mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}\leqslant 4\alpha
.
The map
X\to\mathbf{R}
sending
z
to
\mathchoice{\left\langle x,c\right\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}{\langle x,c\rangle_{z}}
is continuous, hence
(iii)
is proved.
Point
(iv)
is obtained in the same way.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>r+15\alpha
.
Let
x,y\in X
.
Assume
(x,y)
has an
(\alpha,L)
-contracting tail.
Then the saturation
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{x}(y,r)
of
\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r)
is a Borel set contained in
\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r+16\alpha)
.

Proof.

As the saturation of a closed subset
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{x}(y,r)
is a Borel set, see
[
9
, Lemma 3.7]
.
Let
\gamma
be a contracting tail of
(x,y)
.
By assumption there is a projection
p
of
x
on
\gamma
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(p,y\right)}{d(p,y)}{d(p,y)}{d(p,y)}\geqslant L
.
Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\partial X
be two equivalent cocycles with
c\in\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r)
.
Denote by
q
and
q^{\prime}
respective projections of
c
and
c^{\prime}
on
\gamma
.
According to
Lemma 3.15
,
\mathchoice{d\left(y,q\right)}{d(y,q)}{d(y,q)}{d(y,q)}\leqslant r+7\alpha
.
Combined with
Lemma 3.11
we get
\mathchoice{d\left(y,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(y,q^{\prime})}{d(y,q^{\prime})}{d(y,q^{\prime})}\leqslant r+11\alpha
.
Consequently

\mathchoice{d\left(p,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(p,q^{\prime})}{d(p,q^{\prime})}{d(p,q^{\prime})}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(p,y\right)}{d(p,y)}{d(p,y)}{d(p,y)}-\mathchoice{d\left(q^{\prime},y\right)}{d(q^{\prime},y)}{d(q^{\prime},y)}{d(q^{\prime},y)}\geqslant L-(r+11\alpha)>4\alpha.

Combining
Lemma 3.9
with the triangle inequality we see that

\mathchoice{\left\langle x,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}{\langle x,c^{\prime}\rangle_{y}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle x,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{q^{\prime}}}{\langle x,c^{\prime}\rangle_{q^{\prime}}}{\langle x,c^{\prime}\rangle_{q^{\prime}}}{\langle x,c^{\prime}\rangle_{q^{\prime}}}+\mathchoice{d\left(q^{\prime},y\right)}{d(q^{\prime},y)}{d(q^{\prime},y)}{d(q^{\prime},y)}\leqslant r+16\alpha.

Hence
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}_{x}(y,r+16\alpha)
.
∎

Definition 3.17
.

Let
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Let
y,y^{\prime}\in X
.
Two subsets
Z,Z^{\prime}\subset\bar{X}
are
r
-separated by the pair
(y,y^{\prime})
, if

Z\subset\mathcal{O}_{y^{\prime}}(y,r)\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{O}_{y}(y^{\prime},r)

If
Z=\{z\}
and
Z^{\prime}=\{z^{\prime}\}
are reduced to a point, we simply that
z
and
z^{\prime}
are
r
-separated by
(y,y^{\prime})
.

Recall that shadows are closed subsets of
\bar{X}
.
Hence being
r
-separated by
(y,y^{\prime})
is a closed condition on
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
and
r\in\mathbf{R}
.
Let
y,y^{\prime}\in X
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}>2r+21\alpha
.
Assume that there exists an
\alpha
-contracting geodesic
\gamma\colon\mathchoice{\left[a\,,a^{\prime}\right]}{[a\,,a^{\prime}]}{[a\,,a^{\prime}]}{[a\,,a^{\prime}]}\to X
joining
y
to
y^{\prime}
.
Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
.
Let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be respective projections of
c
and
c^{\prime}
on
\gamma
.
If
c
and
c^{\prime}
are
r
-separated by
(y,y^{\prime})
then the following holds.

(i)

\max\{\mathchoice{d\left(q,y\right)}{d(q,y)}{d(q,y)}{d(q,y)},\mathchoice{d\left(q^{\prime},y^{\prime}\right)}{d(q^{\prime},y^{\prime})}{d(q^{\prime},y^{\prime})}{d(q^{\prime},y^{\prime})}\}\leqslant r+7\alpha
.

(ii)

There is a complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.

(iii)

For any complete gradient arc
\nu\colon I\to X
from
c
to
c^{\prime}
, we have
d(\nu,\gamma)<\alpha
.
Moreover the entry point ( respectively exit point) of
\nu
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(\gamma)
is
5\alpha
-close to
q
( respectively
q^{\prime}
).

(iv)

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,\gamma(s^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}{\langle c,\gamma(s^{\prime})\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}{\langle c,\gamma(s^{\prime})\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}{\langle c,\gamma(s^{\prime})\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}\leqslant 2\alpha
, for every
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathchoice{\left[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}\right]}{[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}]}{[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}]}{[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}]}
with
s^{\prime}\geqslant s
.

(v)

\mathchoice{\left\langle c,c^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}{\langle c,c^{\prime}\rangle_{\gamma(s)}}\leqslant 4\alpha
, for every
s\in\mathchoice{\left[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+7\alpha)\right]}{[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+7\alpha)]}{[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+7\alpha)]}{[a+(r+7\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+7\alpha)]}
.

(vi)

The path
\gamma
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[a+(r+13\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+13\alpha)\right]}{[a+(r+13\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+13\alpha)]}{[a+(r+13\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+13\alpha)]}{[a+(r+13\alpha)\,,a^{\prime}-(r+13\alpha)]}
lies in the
5\alpha
-neighborhood of any complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Note that both
(y,y^{\prime})
and
(y^{\prime},y)
have an
(\alpha,L)
-contracting tail.
Hence
(i)
is just a consequence of
Lemma 3.15
.
Moreover

\mathchoice{d\left(q,q^{\prime}\right)}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}{d(q,q^{\prime})}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})}-2r-14\alpha>7\alpha.

Hence
(ii)
and
(iii)
follow from
Lemma 3.10
.
Point
(iv)
is a particular case of
Lemma 3.15
while
(v)
is proved exactly in the same way.
We are left to prove
(vi)
.
For simplicity let
b=a+(r+13\alpha)
and
b^{\prime}=a^{\prime}-(r+13\alpha)
.
Let
\nu
be a complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
.
Denote by
p=\nu(t)
and
p^{\prime}=\nu(t^{\prime})
the respective entry and exit points of
\nu
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(\gamma)
.
In view of
(i)
and
(iii)
,
p
and
p^{\prime}
are respectively
\alpha
-close to
m=\gamma(s)
and
m^{\prime}=\gamma(s^{\prime})
for some
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathchoice{\left[a\,,b\right]}{[a\,,b]}{[a\,,b]}{[a\,,b]}\times\mathchoice{\left[b^{\prime}\,,a^{\prime}\right]}{[b^{\prime}\,,a^{\prime}]}{[b^{\prime}\,,a^{\prime}]}{[b^{\prime}\,,a^{\prime}]}
.
Thus
\nu
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[t\,,t^{\prime}\right]}{[t\,,t^{\prime}]}{[t\,,t^{\prime}]}{[t\,,t^{\prime}]}
lies in the
(5\alpha/2)
-neighborhood of
\gamma
.
It follows then from
[
9
, Lemma 5.1]
that
\gamma
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[s\,,s^{\prime}\right]}{[s\,,s^{\prime}]}{[s\,,s^{\prime}]}{[s\,,s^{\prime}]}
lies in the
5\alpha
-neighborhood of
\nu
, whence the result.
∎

3.6
Conformal densities

Definition 3.19

(Density)
.

Let
G
be a group acting properly by isometries on
X
.
Fix a base point
o\in X
.
Let
\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
A
density
is a collection
\nu=(\nu_{x})
of positive finite measures on
(\bar{X},\mathfrak{B})
indexed by
X
such that
\nu_{x}\ll\nu_{y}
, for every
x,y\in X,
and normalized by
\mathchoice{\left\|\nu_{o}\right\|}{\|\nu_{o}\|}{\|\nu_{o}\|}{\|\nu_{o}\|}=1
.
Such a density is

(i)

G
-invariant
, if
g_{\ast}\nu_{x}=\nu_{gx}
, for every
g\in G
and
x\in X
;

(ii)

\omega
-conformal
, if for every
x,y\in X
,

\frac{d\nu_{x}}{d\nu_{y}}(c)=e^{-\omega c(x,y)},\quad\nu_{y}-\text{a.s.}

Existence of
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal densities supported on
\partial X
follows from the Patterson construction, see
[
9
, Proposition 4.3]
.
The Shadow Lemma stated below is a key tool in the study of invariant, conformal densities, , see
[
9
, Corollary 4.10]
.

Proposition 3.20

(Shadow Lemma)
.

Assume that
G
is not virtually cyclic and has a contracting element.
Let
\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
There exists
\varepsilon,r_{0}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that for every
r\geqslant r_{0}
, for every
G
-invariant,
\omega
-conformal density
\nu=(\nu_{x})
, for every
g\in G
, we have

\varepsilon e^{-\omega\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}}\leqslant\nu_{o}\left(\mathcal{O}_{o}(go,r)\right)\leqslant e^{2\omega r}e^{-\omega\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}}.

Atoms.

Let
\nu=(\nu_{x})
be a
G
-invariant density on
\bar{X}
.
We collect here a few facts about the atoms of the measure space
(\bar{X},\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
.
For such an atom
A
, its
stabilizer
is the set

\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(A\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}=\mathchoice{\left\{h\in G\;:\ \nu_{o}(A\cap hA)=\nu_{o}(A)\right\}}{\{h\in G\;:\ \nu_{o}(A\cap hA)=\nu_{o}(A)\}}{\{h\in G\;:\ \nu_{o}(A\cap hA)=\nu_{o}(A)\}}{\{h\in G\;:\ \nu_{o}(A\cap hA)=\nu_{o}(A)\}}.

As the terminology suggests, it is a subgroup of
G
.
Indeed, if
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
belong to
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(A\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}
, then,

\nu_{o}(h_{1}A\cap h_{2}A)\geqslant\nu_{o}\left((A\cap h_{1}A)\cap(A\cap h_{2}A)\right)\geqslant\nu_{o}(A)>0.

As
\nu
is
G
-invariant, its yields
\nu_{o}(A\cap h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}A)>0
.
However
A
is an atom, thus
h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}\in\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(A\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
g\in G
be a contracting element and
Y
an axis of
g
.
There is
D\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with the following property:
for every atom
A
of
(\bar{X},\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
, there is an atom
B\subset A\setminus\partial^{+}Y
such that the projection of
B
on
Y
has diameter at most
D
.

Proof.

Whithout loss of generality, we can take for
Y
the
\left\langle h\right\rangle
-orbit of some point
x
.
There is
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that
Y
is
\alpha
-contracting.
Up to increasing the value of
\alpha
, we can assume that any geodesic joining two points of
Y
is
\alpha
-contracting; moreover for every
p,q,n\in\mathbf{Z}
, with
p\leqslant q\leqslant n
, the point
g^{q}x
lies in the
\alpha
-neighborhood of any geodesic from
g^{p}x
to
g^{n}x
.
According to
Lemma 3.16
, for every
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, there is
N(r)\in\mathbf{N}
with the following property: for every
n\in\mathbf{Z}
, with
\mathchoice{\left|n\right|}{|n|}{|n|}{|n|}\geqslant N(r)
we have

\mathcal{O}_{o}(g^{n}o,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{o}^{+}(g^{n}o,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{o}(g^{n}o,r+16\alpha).

Recall that
\mathcal{O}_{o}^{+}(g^{n}o,r)
stands for the saturation of
\mathcal{O}_{o}(g^{n}o,r)
.
In view of the Shadow Lemma (
Proposition 3.20
) there is
r\geqslant 100\alpha
and
n\geqslant N(r)
such that

\nu_{o}(\mathcal{O}_{o}(g^{-n}o,r+16\alpha))<\nu_{o}(A)\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{o}(\mathcal{O}_{o}(g^{n}o,r+16\alpha))<\nu_{o}(A)

Since
A
is an atom, it forces the set

A_{0}=A\setminus\left(O_{o}^{+}(g^{-n}o,r)\cup O_{o}^{+}(g^{n}o,r)\right)

to have full measure in
A
.
In particular, it is also an atom.
For every
k\in\mathbf{Z}
, we write
A_{0}(k)
for the set of all cocycles
c\in A_{0}
such that
g^{k}x
is a projection of
c
on
Y
.
Recall that
r\geqslant 100\alpha
.
Since
g
is contracting,
\partial^{+}Y
is contained in
O_{o}(g^{-n}o,r)\cup O_{o}(g^{n}o,r)
, therefore
A_{0}
is covered by the union of all
A_{0}(k)
.
By construction
A_{0}(k)
is a closed subset, hence its saturation
A_{0}^{+}(k)
is Borel, see
[
9
, Lemma 3.7]
.
As
A_{0}
is an atom of
(\bar{X},\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
, there is
k\in\mathbf{Z}
such that
B=A^{+}_{0}(k)
has full measure in
A_{0}
(hence in
A
) and thus is an atom.
According to
Lemma 3.11
, the projection
p
of any cocycle in
B
satisfies
\mathchoice{d\left(p,g^{k}o\right)}{d(p,g^{k}o)}{d(p,g^{k}o)}{d(p,g^{k}o)}\leqslant 4\alpha
.
Hence
\pi_{Y}(B)
has diameter at most
8\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
A
be an atom of
(\bar{X},\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
.
Then
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(A\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}
does not contain a contracting element.

Proof.

Let
g\in G
be a contracting element and
Y
an axis of
g
.
According to
Lemma 3.21
, we can find an atom
B\subset A
and
m\in\mathbf{N}
, such that the projections on
Y
of
B
and
g^{m}B
are disjoint.
Hence so are
B
and
g^{m}B
.
Recall that
\nu
is
G
-invariant.
Hence
B
and
g^{m}B
have full measure in
A
and
g^{m}A
respectively.
This forces
\nu_{o}(A\cap g^{m}A)=0
, i.e.
g^{m}
, and thus
g
, does not belong to
\mathchoice{\operatorname{Stab}\left(A\right)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}{\operatorname{Stab}(A)}
.
∎

4
Radial limit sets

In this section,
G
is still a group acting properly by isometries on a proper geodesic metric space
(X,d)
.
By analogy with hyperbolic geometry, we define the radial limit set for the action of
G
on
X
.
We give here a unified approach for the various limit sets studied in
[
9
]
and in the present article.

4.1
Definition and examples

Denote by
2^{G}
the collection of all subsets of
G
.
Consider a non-increasing map
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
, that is such that
U(L^{\prime})\subset U(L)
whenever
L^{\prime}\geqslant L
.
Let
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
The set
\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r,L)
consists of all cocycles
c\in\partial X
with the following property:
for every
T\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, there is
g\in U(L)
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\geqslant T
and
c\in\mathcal{O}_{o}(go,r)
.
We also let

\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r)=\bigcap_{L\geqslant 0}\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r,L)=\bigcap_{L,T\geqslant 0}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in U(L)\\
\mathchoice{d\left(go,o\right)}{d(go,o)}{d(go,o)}{d(go,o)}\geqslant T\end{subarray}}\mathcal{O}_{o}(go,r).

Definition 4.1
.

The
U
-radial limit set
of
G
is

\Lambda_{U}(G)=\bigcup_{r\geqslant 0}G\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r)

The set
\Lambda_{U}(G)
is a
G
-invariant saturated Borel subset.
Invariance follows from the definition, while saturation is a consequence of (
5
).
It follows also from (
5
) that
\Lambda_{U}(G)
does not depend on the choice of the base point
o
.

Remark 4.1
.

Let
U_{1},U_{2}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
be two non-increasing maps.
Denote by
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
the map given by
U(L)=U_{1}(L)\cup U_{2}(L)
for every
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
One checks that

\Lambda_{U}(G)=\Lambda_{U_{1}}(G)\cup\Lambda_{U_{2}}(G).

In particular, if
U_{1}(L)\subset U_{2}(L)
for every
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, then
\Lambda_{U_{1}}(G)\subset\Lambda_{U_{2}}(G)
.
∎

Example 4.1
.

(i)

If
U
is the constant map such that
U(L)=G
, then we recover the
radial limit set
, which we denote by
\Lambda_{\rm rad}(G)
, see
[
9
, Definition 4.20]
.
According to the above remark, it contains all other
U
-radial limit sets.

(ii)

Let
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}^{*}_{+}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}(L)
the set of elements
g\in G
such that
(o,go)
has an
(\alpha,L)
-contracting tail (see
Definition 3.14
).
Then the union

\Lambda_{\rm ctg}(G)=\bigcup_{\alpha\geqslant 0}\Lambda_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}}(G)

is the
contracting limit set
studied in
[
9
, Section 5.2]
.

Another example is detailed in
Section 4.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
c\in\Lambda_{\rm ctg}(G)
.
Let
(x_{n})
be a sequence of points in
X
.
Assume that there is
M\in\mathbf{R}
, such that for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we have
c(o,x_{n})\geqslant M
.
Then any accumulation point of
(x_{n})
in
\partial X
is equivalent to
c
.

Proof.

By definition, there are
\alpha,r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
, and
g\in G
, such that
g^{-1}c\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}}(G,o,r)
.
For simplicity we write
b_{n}=\iota(x_{n})
for the cocycle associated to
x_{n}
.
Up to passing to a subsequence we assume that
(b_{n})
converges to a point
b^{*}\in\partial X
.
In particular,
(x_{n})
leaves every bounded subset.

We fix now
R\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and denote by
K
the closed ball of radius
R
centered at
o
.
Let
L>r+28\alpha
.
There is
u\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}(L)
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}>R+r+28\alpha
and
g^{-1}c\in\mathcal{O}_{o}(uo,r)
.
By definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
, there is an
\alpha
-contracting geodesic
\tau
ending at
uo
and a projection
p
of
o
on
\tau
satisfying
\mathchoice{d\left(p,uo\right)}{d(p,uo)}{d(p,uo)}{d(p,uo)}\geqslant L
.
Denote by
q
a projection of
g^{-1}c
on
\tau
and
q_{n}
a projection of
g^{-1}x_{n}
on
\tau
.
Observe that
\mathchoice{d\left(q_{n},q\right)}{d(q_{n},q)}{d(q_{n},q)}{d(q_{n},q)}\leqslant 4\alpha
, for all but finitely many
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
Indeed, if this is not the case, then it follows from
Lemma 3.9
that

\displaystyle M\leqslant c(o,x_{n})\leqslant c(o,gq)+c(gq,x_{n})

\displaystyle\leqslant c(o,gq)+[g^{-1}c]\left(q,g^{-1}x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant c(o,gq)-\mathchoice{d\left(x_{n},gq\right)}{d(x_{n},gq)}{d(x_{n},gq)}{d(x_{n},gq)}+14\alpha,

which contradicts the fact that
(x_{n})
leaves every bounded subset.
By
Lemma 3.15

\mathchoice{d\left(uo,q\right)}{d(uo,q)}{d(uo,q)}{d(uo,q)}\leqslant r+7\alpha
, hence
\mathchoice{d\left(uo,q_{n}\right)}{d(uo,q_{n})}{d(uo,q_{n})}{d(uo,q_{n})}\leqslant r+11\alpha
.
Recall that
p
is a projection of
o
on
\tau
.
Moreover

\mathchoice{d\left(p,q_{n}\right)}{d(p,q_{n})}{d(p,q_{n})}{d(p,q_{n})}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(p,uo\right)}{d(p,uo)}{d(p,uo)}{d(p,uo)}-\mathchoice{d\left(uo,q_{n}\right)}{d(uo,q_{n})}{d(uo,q_{n})}{d(uo,q_{n})}\geqslant L-(r+11\alpha)>\alpha

It follows them from
Lemma 2.3
that

\mathchoice{\left\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\right\rangle_{uo}}{\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\rangle_{uo}}{\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\rangle_{uo}}{\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\rangle_{uo}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\right\rangle_{q_{n}}}{\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\rangle_{q_{n}}}{\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\rangle_{q_{n}}}{\langle o,g^{-1}x_{n}\rangle_{q_{n}}}+\mathchoice{d\left(q_{n},uo\right)}{d(q_{n},uo)}{d(q_{n},uo)}{d(q_{n},uo)}\leqslant r+15\alpha.

Thus
g^{-1}x_{n}
belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{o}(uo,r+15\alpha)
as
g^{-1}c
does.
We assumed that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}>R+r+28\alpha
.
Hence by
[
9
, Lemma 5.7]
we have
\mathchoice{\left\|c-b_{n}\right\|_{gK}}{\|c-b_{n}\|_{gK}}{\|c-b_{n}\|_{gK}}{\|c-b_{n}\|_{gK}}\leqslant 20\alpha
, for all but finitely many
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
In particular, since
b^{*}
is an accumulation point of
(b_{n})
, we have
\mathchoice{\left\|c-b^{*}\right\|_{gK}}{\|c-b^{*}\|_{gK}}{\|c-b^{*}\|_{gK}}{\|c-b^{*}\|_{gK}}\leqslant 20\alpha
.
The above estimate holds for every closed ball
K
centered at
o
, thus
c
and
b^{*}
are equivalent.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
c\in\Lambda_{\rm ctg}(G)
.
Let
(x_{n})
be a sequence of points in
X
such that
c(o,x_{n})
diverges to infinity.
Then any accumulation point of
(x_{n})
in
\bar{X}
is equivalent to
c
.

Proof.

Since cocycles in
\bar{X}
are
1
-Lipschitz, we observe that no subsequence of
(x_{n})
is bounded.
Hence, any accumulation point of
(x_{n})
belongs to
\partial X
.
The result now follows from
Proposition 4.2

∎

4.2
Measure of radial limit sets

Assume now that
G
contains a contracting element for its action on
X
.
If the action of
G
on
X
is divergent (i.e. the Poincaré series of
G
diverges at the critical exponent
s=\omega_{G}
), it is a “well-known” fact that any
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal density is supported on the radial limit set.
More precisely we proved the following statement, which can be understood as a partial form of the Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy. See Corollaries 4.25 and 5.19 as well as Proposition 5.22 in
[
9
]
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
G
be a group acting properly by isometries on a proper geodesic space
X
.
Assume that
G
is not virtually cyclic and contains a contracting element.
Let
\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and
\nu=(\nu_{x})
be a
G
-invariant,
\omega
-conformal density.
The followings are equivalent.

(i)

The Poincaré series
\mathcal{P}_{G}(s)
diverges at
s=\omega
(hence
\omega=\omega_{G}
).

(ii)

\nu_{o}
gives positive measure to the radial limit set

(iii)

\nu_{o}
is supported on the contracting limit set
\Lambda_{\rm ctg}(G)
, hence on the radial limit set
\Lambda_{\rm rad}(G)
.

In this situation, the measure
\nu_{o}
restricted to
(\bar{X},\mathfrak{B}^{+})
is non-atomic.

The same line of arguments can be used to prove that several variations of radial limit set have full measure, see
Proposition 4.7
below.
We extract below the main ingredients used in
[
9
]
.

Definition 4.5
.

A non-decreasing map
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
has the
extension property
, if there exists
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that for every
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and
g\in G
, there is
u\in S_{G}(L,r)
with the following properties:
gu\in U(L)
while
go
is
r
-close to some geodesic from
o
to
guo
.

Definition 4.6
.

A non-decreasing map
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
is
almost
G
-invariant
, if there exists
r_{1}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that for every
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>r+r_{1}
, for every
u\in G
, there is
T\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that for every
g\in U(L+r_{1})
with
\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\geqslant T
, the following holds

(i)

ug\in U(L)
,

(ii)

u\mathcal{O}_{o}(go,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{o}(ugo,r+r_{1})
.

The last definition is designed to control the
G
-invariance of the limit set.
More precisely, if
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
is an almost
G
-invariant, non-decreasing map, then for every
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>r+r_{1}
,

G\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r,L+r_{1})\subset\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r+r_{1},L),

(it suffices to unwrap all definitions) and thus

G\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r)\subset\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r+r_{1}).

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\nu=(\nu_{x})
be a
G
-invariant
\omega_{G}
conformal density.
Let
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
be an almost
G
-invariant, non-decreasing map with the extension property.
Assume that there is
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}^{*}_{+}
such that for all
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we have
U(L)\subset\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}(L)
.
If
G
is not virtually cyclic and the action of
G
on
X
is divergent then there exists
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that
\nu_{o}(\Lambda_{U}(G,o,r))=1
.
In particular,
\nu_{o}
is supported on
\Lambda_{U}(G)
.

Proof.

The proof goes verbatim as in
[
9
, Section 5]
.
∎

4.3
Radial limit set associated to a contracting element

We provide here another useful variation of the contracting limit set defined previously.
Fix a contracting element
h\in G
.
Choose a bi-infinite geodesic
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to X
, such that the Hausdorff distance between
\gamma
and some (hence any) orbit of
\left\langle h\right\rangle
is finite, and set
o=\gamma(0)
.
The path
\gamma
yields two Busemann cocycles
c_{\gamma},c^{\prime}_{\gamma}\in\partial X
(see
Example 3.1
).
We assume that
\gamma
is oriented so that
c^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is equivalent to the some (hence any) accumulation point of
(h^{n}o)_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
in
\partial X
.
There is
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that any subpath of
\gamma
is
\alpha
-contracting.

We define a map
U_{\gamma}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
as follows: given
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, the subset
U_{\gamma}(L)\subset G
consists of all elements
g\in G
for which there is
t\in[L,\infty)
such that
g\gamma(-t)
is a projection of
o
onto
g\gamma
.
Note that
U_{\gamma}(L)\subset\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}(L)
, for every
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Hence the corresponding limit set
\Lambda_{U_{\gamma}}(G)
is contained in the contracting limit set.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
be two bi-infinite geodesic.
Assume that there exists
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
,
T\in\mathbf{R}
and
D\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that

\mathchoice{d\left(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t+T)\right)}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t+T))}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t+T))}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t+T))}\leqslant D,\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

Let
I_{1}\subset\mathbf{R}
be a closed interval and
I_{2}=\mathchoice{\left\{\varepsilon t+T\;:\ t\in I_{1}\right\}}{\{\varepsilon t+T\;:\ t\in I_{1}\}}{\{\varepsilon t+T\;:\ t\in I_{1}\}}{\{\varepsilon t+T\;:\ t\in I_{1}\}}
.
Denote by
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
, the path
\gamma_{i}
restricted to
I_{i}
.
Let
x\in X
.
Let
p_{i}=\gamma_{i}(t_{i})
be a projection of
x
on
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
.
If
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
is
\alpha
-contracting, then
\mathchoice{\left|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)\right|}{|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)|}{|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)|}{|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)|}\leqslant 2D+4\alpha
.

Proof.

On the one hand,
Lemma 2.3
gives

\displaystyle\mathchoice{d\left(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)\right)}{d(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}{d(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}{d(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,p_{2}\right)}{d(x,p_{2})}{d(x,p_{2})}{d(x,p_{2})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p_{2},\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)\right)}{d(p_{2},\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}{d(p_{2},\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}{d(p_{2},\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}-4\alpha

\displaystyle\geqslant d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{2})+\mathchoice{\left|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)\right|}{|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)|}{|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)|}{|t_{2}-(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)|}-4\alpha.

On the other hand, it follows from our assumption that

\mathchoice{d\left(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T)\right)}{d(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}{d(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}{d(x,\gamma_{2}(\varepsilon t_{1}+T))}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,p_{1}\right)}{d(x,p_{1})}{d(x,p_{1})}{d(x,p_{1})}+D\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\right)}{d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{1})}{d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{1})}{d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{1})}+D\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\right)}{d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{2})}{d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{2})}{d(x,\gamma^{\prime}_{2})}+2D.

The result follows from the combination of these two observations.
∎

Combining
Lemma 4.8
with (
6
) one checks that the limit set
\Lambda_{U_{\gamma}}(G)
only depends on
h
and not on the choice of
\gamma
.
For simplicity, we denote this set by
\Lambda_{h}
.

Remark 4.2
.

Note that the orientation of
\gamma
matters in the definition of
U_{\gamma}
.
Therefore
\Lambda_{h}
is a priori distinct from
\Lambda_{h^{-1}}
, which would be defined using the map
U_{\bar{\gamma}}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
associated to the reverse path
\bar{\gamma}
, that is the path given by
\bar{\gamma}(t)=\gamma(-t)
, for every
t\in\mathbf{R}
.
∎

The set
\Lambda_{h}
is a
G
-invariant, saturated Borel subset of
\partial X
.
We now claim that any
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal density is supported on
\Lambda_{h}
.
To that end it suffices to check the assumptions of
Proposition 4.7
.
The first one is a variation on
[
9
, Lemma 4.16]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

If that
G
is not virtually cyclic, then the map
U_{\gamma}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
satisfies the extension property.

Proof.

Since
G
is not virtually cyclic, there is
s\in G\setminus E(h)
.
In particular,
\pi_{\gamma}(s\gamma)
has diameter at most
D_{0}
for some
D_{0}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Let
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and
g\in G
.
In order to simplify the exposition, in the remainder of this proof we denote by
D_{i}
parameters which do not depend on
L
and
g
.
Their value could be estimated in terms of
\alpha
,
\mathchoice{d\left(o,ho\right)}{d(o,ho)}{d(o,ho)}{d(o,ho)}
,
\mathchoice{d\left(o,so\right)}{d(o,so)}{d(o,so)}{d(o,so)}
, etc, but these computations will not improve the qualitative nature of the statement.

Since
\gamma
and
s\gamma
are both
\alpha
-contracting, with a small “overlap”, one checks that if
\gamma(t_{0})
and
\gamma^{\prime}(t^{\prime}_{0})
are projections of
g^{-1}o
on
\gamma
and
s\gamma
respectively, then

\max\{t_{0},t^{\prime}_{0}\}\leqslant D_{1}.

Assume that
t^{\prime}_{0}\leqslant D_{1}
(the other case works in the same way).
Since
h
is contracting, there is
D_{2}
and
u\in s\left\langle h\right\rangle
such that

\mathchoice{d\left(u\gamma(t),s\gamma(t+L)\right)}{d(u\gamma(t),s\gamma(t+L))}{d(u\gamma(t),s\gamma(t+L))}{d(u\gamma(t),s\gamma(t+L))}\leqslant D_{2},\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

In particular,

\mathchoice{\left|\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}-L\right|}{|\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}-L|}{|\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}-L|}{|\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}-L|}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,so\right)}{d(o,so)}{d(o,so)}{d(o,so)}+\mathchoice{d\left(s\gamma(L),u\gamma(0)\right)}{d(s\gamma(L),u\gamma(0))}{d(s\gamma(L),u\gamma(0))}{d(s\gamma(L),u\gamma(0))}\leqslant D_{3},

Hence
u\in S_{G}(L,D_{3})
.
In addition the projection
u\gamma(t^{\prime}_{1})
of
g^{-1}o
on
u\gamma
is such that
\mathchoice{\left|t^{\prime}_{1}-(t^{\prime}_{0}-L)\right|}{|t^{\prime}_{1}-(t^{\prime}_{0}-L)|}{|t^{\prime}_{1}-(t^{\prime}_{0}-L)|}{|t^{\prime}_{1}-(t^{\prime}_{0}-L)|}\leqslant D_{4}
(
Lemma 4.8
).
Hence
gu\in U_{\gamma}(L-D_{5})
.
Since
\gamma
is contracting,
u\gamma(t^{\prime}_{1})
is
D_{6}
-close to any geodesic from
g^{-1}o
to
uo
.
The point
u\gamma(t^{\prime}_{1})
is
D_{7}
-close to
s\gamma(t^{\prime}_{0})
while
t^{\prime}_{0}\leqslant D_{1}
.
We deduce that
o
is
D_{8}
-close to any geodesic from
g^{-1}o
to
uo
.
Hence
go
is
D_{8}
-close to any geodesic from
o
to
guo
.
The argument works for any
g\in G
and
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, whence the result.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

The map
U_{\gamma}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
is almost
G
-invariant.

Proof.

In this proof, we denote by
\gamma_{-}
the path
\gamma
restricted to
(-\infty,0]
.
We let
r_{1}=14\alpha
.
Let
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>r+r_{1}
and
u\in G
.
We fix
T>\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}+L+6\alpha
.
Let
g\in U_{\gamma}(L+r_{1})
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\geqslant T
.
By definition there a projection
p
of
o
on
g\gamma_{-}
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(p,go\right)}{d(p,go)}{d(p,go)}{d(p,go)}\geqslant L+r_{1}
.
Let

be a projection of

on
g\gamma_{-}
.
We claim that
\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},go\right)}{d(p^{\prime},go)}{d(p^{\prime},go)}{d(p^{\prime},go)}\geqslant L
.
Assume on the contrary that it is not the case.
In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}>\alpha
.
Since
g\gamma_{-}

-contracting we get from

\displaystyle\mathchoice{d\left(o,u^{-1}o\right)}{d(o,u^{-1}o)}{d(o,u^{-1}o)}{d(o,u^{-1}o)}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p\right)}{d(o,p)}{d(o,p)}{d(o,p)}+\mathchoice{d\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}-6\alpha

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p\right)}{d(o,p)}{d(o,p)}{d(o,p)}+\left[\mathchoice{d\left(p,go\right)}{d(p,go)}{d(p,go)}{d(p,go)}-\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},go\right)}{d(p^{\prime},go)}{d(p^{\prime},go)}{d(p^{\prime},go)}\right]-6\alpha

This contradicts the fact that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}\geqslant T
, and complete the proof of our claim.
In particular,

belongs to

Let

.
Denote by

on

Since

, we know by

\mathchoice{d\left(q,go\right)}{d(q,go)}{d(q,go)}{d(q,go)}\leqslant r+7\alpha
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},q\right)}{d(p^{\prime},q)}{d(p^{\prime},q)}{d(p^{\prime},q)}>4\alpha
.
Recall that

and

and

on

It follows from

\mathchoice{\left\langle u^{-1}o,c\right\rangle_{q}}{\langle u^{-1}o,c\rangle_{q}}{\langle u^{-1}o,c\rangle_{q}}{\langle u^{-1}o,c\rangle_{q}}\leqslant 5\alpha
.
The triangle inequality now yields
\mathchoice{\left\langle u^{-1}o,c\right\rangle_{go}}{\langle u^{-1}o,c\rangle_{go}}{\langle u^{-1}o,c\rangle_{go}}{\langle u^{-1}o,c\rangle_{go}}\leqslant r+12\alpha
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,uc\right\rangle_{ugo}}{\langle o,uc\rangle_{ugo}}{\langle o,uc\rangle_{ugo}}{\langle o,uc\rangle_{ugo}}\leqslant r+12\alpha

We just proved that
u\mathcal{O}_{o}(go,r)\subset\mathcal{O}_{o}(ugo,r+r_{1})
, whence the result.
∎

The next statement is now just a particular case of

.

Proposition 4.11
.

Assume that
G
is not virtually cyclic and the action of
G
on
X
is divergent.
Then for every
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal density
\nu=(\nu_{x})

\nu_{o}(\Lambda_{h})=1
.

We complete this section with additional properties of

.

Proposition 4.12
.

There is
r_{0}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that the set
\Lambda_{h}
coincides with
\Lambda_{U_{\gamma}}(G,o,r_{0})
.

Proof.

By construction

is contained in

for every

.
Hence it suffices to prove the converse inclusion for a suitable value of

Since

is contracting, there is

such that for every

, there is
u\in\left\langle h\right\rangle
, with the following property:

\mathchoice{d\left(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T)\right)}{d(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}{d(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}{d(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}\leqslant D,\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

.
Since

is almost

), we know that

is contained in

\bigcup_{r\geqslant 0}\Lambda_{U_{\gamma}}(G,o,r)=\bigcup_{r\geqslant 0}\bigcap_{L\geqslant 0}\Lambda_{U_{\gamma}}(G,o,r,L).

Let

.
There is
r\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that
c\in\Lambda_{U_{\gamma}}(G,o,r)
.
We fix
T=r+7\alpha
.
According to our choice of

, there is
u\in\left\langle h\right\rangle
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T)\right)}{d(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}{d(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}{d(u\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}\leqslant D

Let

and

Since

In addition,

∎

Proposition 4.13
.

Let

, where

.

Remark 4.3
.

In particular,

.

∎

Proof.

Since

is contracting, there is
D\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, such that for every

, there is

, such that

\mathchoice{d\left(h^{n}\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T)\right)}{d(h^{n}\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}{d(h^{n}\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}{d(h^{n}\gamma(t),\gamma(t-T))}\leqslant D,\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

(7)

In addition, we write

for the parameter given by

and let

.
Let

.
Set

.
As explained above, there is

such that (

) holds.

According to

We are now going to prove that

Since

belongs to
\Lambda_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}}(G)
, its equivalence class is

see
[

, Proposition 5.10]
.
In particular, it is a closed subset of

.
Thus for any

, there is a neighborhood

of

.
We claim that for all but finitely many

, for all

, any projection

of

onto

restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[-L\,,0\right]}{[-L\,,0]}{[-L\,,0]}{[-L\,,0]}
, satisfies
\mathchoice{d\left(g_{k}o,q\right)}{d(g_{k}o,q)}{d(g_{k}o,q)}{d(g_{k}o,q)}\geqslant L-18\alpha
.
Suppose on the contrary that it is not the case.
Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
, there is
b_{k}\in V_{c}
a projection
q_{k}
is a projection of
b_{k}
onto
g_{k}\gamma
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[-L\,,0\right]}{[-L\,,0]}{[-L\,,0]}{[-L\,,0]}

\mathchoice{d\left(g_{k}o,q_{k}\right)}{d(g_{k}o,q_{k})}{d(g_{k}o,q_{k})}{d(g_{k}o,q_{k})}<L-18\alpha
.
Moreover we can suppose that
(b_{k})
converges to a cocycle

in the closure of

.
Recall that
g_{k}
belongs to
U_{\gamma}(L)
.
Hence there is

such that

is a projection of

on
g_{k}\gamma
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(p_{k},q_{k}\right)}{d(p_{k},q_{k})}{d(p_{k},q_{k})}{d(p_{k},q_{k})}>4\alpha
.
Using
Lemma 3.9
we observe that
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,b_{k}\right\rangle_{q_{k}}}{\langle o,b_{k}\rangle_{q_{k}}}{\langle o,b_{k}\rangle_{q_{k}}}{\langle o,b_{k}\rangle_{q_{k}}}\leqslant 5\alpha
.
Hence
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,b_{k}\right\rangle_{g_{k}o}}{\langle o,b_{k}\rangle_{g_{k}o}}{\langle o,b_{k}\rangle_{g_{k}o}}{\langle o,b_{k}\rangle_{g_{k}o}}\leqslant r_{k}
, where
r_{k}=\mathchoice{d\left(g_{k}o,q_{k}\right)}{d(g_{k}o,q_{k})}{d(g_{k}o,q_{k})}{d(g_{k}o,q_{k})}+5\alpha
.
In particular, both
g_{k}o
and

belong to
\mathcal{O}_{o}(g_{k}o,r_{k})
.
Note that
L>r_{k}+13\alpha
.
In view of
[

, Lemma 5.7]
there is a exhaustion of

by a non-decreasing collection of compact subsets

such that for every

, we have
\mathchoice{\left\|b_{k}-\iota(g_{k}o)\right\|_{K_{k}}}{\|b_{k}-\iota(g_{k}o)\|_{K_{k}}}{\|b_{k}-\iota(g_{k}o)\|_{K_{k}}}{\|b_{k}-\iota(g_{k}o)\|_{K_{k}}}\leqslant 20\alpha
.
Recall that any accumulation point

of

is equivalent to

.
Passing to the limit we see that

, hence

is equivalent to

as well.
This contradicts the fact that the closure of

does not intersect

and completes the proof of our claim.

It follows from

, that for all but finitely many

, for all

, we have

\mathchoice{\left\langle b,g_{k}o\right\rangle_{g_{k}\gamma(-L)}}{\langle b,g_{k}o\rangle_{g_{k}\gamma(-L)}}{\langle b,g_{k}o\rangle_{g_{k}\gamma(-L)}}{\langle b,g_{k}o\rangle_{g_{k}\gamma(-L)}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle b,g_{k}o\right\rangle_{q_{k}}}{\langle b,g_{k}o\rangle_{q_{k}}}{\langle b,g_{k}o\rangle_{q_{k}}}{\langle b,g_{k}o\rangle_{q_{k}}}+\left[L-\mathchoice{d\left(q_{k},g_{k}o\right)}{d(q_{k},g_{k}o)}{d(q_{k},g_{k}o)}{d(q_{k},g_{k}o)}\right]\leqslant 23\alpha,

on

\mathchoice{\left[-L\,,0\right]}{[-L\,,0]}{[-L\,,0]}{[-L\,,0]}
.

) and the triangle inequality, it yields

.
Since

is compact it can be covered by finitely many open subsets of the form

where

.
Hence

In other words

and

are

-separated by

.
We conclude with

-separated by

.
∎

5
The reduced horoboundary as a standard measure space

The goal of this section is to prove (among other things) the following statement.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
G
be a group acting properly by isometries on a proper geodesic space
X
, with a contracting element.
There is a
G
-invariant, saturated Borel subset
\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{\rm ctg}(G)
satisfying the following properties.

(i)

If
G
is not virtually cyclic and the action of
G
on
X
is divergent, then for every
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal density
\nu=(\nu_{x})
we have
\nu_{o}(\Lambda)=1
.

(ii)

There is a distance
d_{\infty}
on
\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim
such that the metric topology on
\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim
coincides with the quotient topology induced from
\partial X
.

(iii)

The quotient
(\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim,d_{\infty})
is a separable complete metric space.

Remark 5.1
.

Assume that
G
is not virtually cyclic and the action of
G
on
X
is divergent.
Write
\mathfrak{C}
for the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
(\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim,d_{\infty})
.
Let
\nu
be a
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal density.
Denote by
\mu_{o}
the measure obtained by first restricting
\nu_{o}
to
\Lambda
and then pushing this restriction to
\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim
.
Then
(\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim,\mathfrak{C},\mu_{o})
is a standard probability space.
Moreover,
L^{p}(\partial X,\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
and
L^{p}(\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim,\mathfrak{C},\mu_{o})
are isomorphic, for every
p\in[1,\infty]
.
In this sense
(\partial X,\mathfrak{B}^{+},\nu_{o})
can be treated as a standard measure space.
∎

5.1
Initial data

We assume that
G
contains a contracting element, say
h\in G
.
We choose a bi-infinite geodesic
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
, such that the Hausdorff distance between
\gamma
and some (hence any) orbit of
\left\langle h\right\rangle
is finite.
We suppose that the base point of
X
is
o=\gamma(0)
.
The path
\gamma
yields two Busemann cocycles
c_{\gamma},c^{\prime}_{\gamma}\in\partial X
, as in
Example 3.1
.
We assume that
\gamma
is oriented so that
c^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is equivalent to the some (hence any) accumulation point of
(h^{n}o)_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
in
\partial X
.

Denote by
\bar{\gamma}\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
the reverse path given by
\bar{\gamma}(t)=\gamma(-t)
for every
t\in\mathbf{R}
.
For simplicity we write
U_{-},U_{+}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
for the map
U_{\gamma}
and
U_{\bar{\gamma}}
used in
Section 4.3
.
We define a map
U\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to 2^{G}
by

U(L)=U_{-}(L)\cup U_{+}(L),\quad\forall L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}.

In addition, we let

\Lambda_{-}=\Lambda_{h^{-1}},\quad\Lambda_{+}=\Lambda_{h},\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda=\Lambda_{-}\cup\Lambda_{+}.

(the need of “symmetrizing” the set
\Lambda
as well as the visual distance defined below will appear later, see for instance Lemmas
5.17
and
5.21
).
There is
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
such that the following holds.

(H1)

Any subpath of
\gamma
is
\alpha
-contracting.

(H2)

\mathchoice{d\left(o,ho\right)}{d(o,ho)}{d(o,ho)}{d(o,ho)}\leqslant\alpha
.

(H3)

\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma)\right)}{(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma))}{(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma))}{(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma))}\leqslant\alpha
, for every
g\in G\setminus E(h)
;

(H4)

For every
g\in E(h)
, there is
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
and
T\in\mathbf{R}
such that for every
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we have

\mathchoice{d\left(g\gamma(t),\gamma(\varepsilon t+T)\right)}{d(g\gamma(t),\gamma(\varepsilon t+T))}{d(g\gamma(t),\gamma(\varepsilon t+T))}{d(g\gamma(t),\gamma(\varepsilon t+T))}\leqslant\alpha.

In particular, the Hausdorff distance between
\gamma
and
g\gamma
if at most
\alpha
.

(H5)

Conversely, for every
T\in\mathbf{R}
, there is
g\in\left\langle h\right\rangle
such that for every
t\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, we have
\mathchoice{d\left(g\gamma(t),\gamma(t+T)\right)}{d(g\gamma(t),\gamma(t+T))}{d(g\gamma(t),\gamma(t+T))}{d(g\gamma(t),\gamma(t+T))}\leqslant\alpha
.

These properties follow from the fact that
h
is contracting.
The proofs work as if
h
was a loxodromic element in a hyperbolic space and are left to the reader.
According to
Proposition 4.11
, if
G
is not virtually cyclic and the action of
G
on
X
is divergent, then any
G
-invariant,
\omega_{G}
-conformal density
\nu=(\nu_{x})
gives full measure to
\Lambda_{-}
,
\Lambda_{+}
, and thus
\Lambda
.

Separation.

Vocabulary 5.1
.

In order to shorten the statements, we adopt the following terminology.
Let
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
be two subsets of
\bar{X}
.
Let
r,L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Set
x=\gamma(-\varepsilon L)
and
x^{\prime}=\gamma(\varepsilon L)
.
We say that
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are
(L,r,\varepsilon)
-separated at an element
u\in G
, if
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are
r
-separated by
(ux,ux^{\prime})
, that is

Z\subset\mathcal{O}_{ux^{\prime}}(ux,r)\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{O}_{ux}(ux^{\prime},r).

Remark 5.2
.

Let
r,M\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
M>r+11\alpha
.
Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
According to
Proposition 3.18
, if
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are
(M,r,\varepsilon)
-separated at some
u\in G
, then they are also
(L,2\alpha,\varepsilon)
-separated at
u
, for any
L\leqslant M-(r+7\alpha)
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
r_{1},r_{2},L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L_{i}>r_{i}+32\alpha
.
Let
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
.
Let
c,c^{\prime}\in\bar{X}
.
Assume that
c
and
c^{\prime}
are
(L_{i},r_{i},\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
g_{i}
.
Let
p
be a projection of
g_{1}o
on
g_{2}\gamma
.
If
\mathchoice{d\left(p,g_{1}o\right)}{d(p,g_{1}o)}{d(p,g_{1}o)}{d(p,g_{1}o)}<L_{2}-(r_{2}+22\alpha)
, then
g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}\in E(h)
.

Proof.

Let
x_{i}=g_{i}\gamma(-\varepsilon_{i}L_{i})
and
x_{i}^{\prime}=g_{i}\gamma(\varepsilon_{i}L_{i})
.
Denote by
\nu\colon I\to X
a complete gradient arc from
c
to
c^{\prime}
(such an arc exists by
Proposition 3.18
).
For simplicity we write
\gamma_{i}
for the path
g_{i}\gamma
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[-L_{i}\,,L_{i}\right]}{[-L_{i}\,,L_{i}]}{[-L_{i}\,,L_{i}]}{[-L_{i}\,,L_{i}]}
, so that
\gamma_{i}
joins
x_{i}
to
x^{\prime}_{i}
.
Let
q_{i}
and
q^{\prime}_{i}
be respective projections of
c
and
c^{\prime}
on
\gamma_{i}
.
It follows from
Proposition 3.18

(iii)
that
d(\nu,\gamma_{i})<\alpha
.
Moreover if
\nu(a_{i})
and
\nu(a^{\prime}_{i})
stands for the entry / exit point of
\nu
in
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{i})
then

\mathchoice{d\left(\nu(a_{i}),q_{i}\right)}{d(\nu(a_{i}),q_{i})}{d(\nu(a_{i}),q_{i})}{d(\nu(a_{i}),q_{i})}\leqslant 5\alpha\quad\text{and}\quad\mathchoice{d\left(\nu(a^{\prime}_{i}),q^{\prime}_{i}\right)}{d(\nu(a^{\prime}_{i}),q^{\prime}_{i})}{d(\nu(a^{\prime}_{i}),q^{\prime}_{i})}{d(\nu(a^{\prime}_{i}),q^{\prime}_{i})}\leqslant 5\alpha.

(8)

In addition,
\nu
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[a_{i}\,,a^{\prime}_{i}\right]}{[a_{i}\,,a^{\prime}_{i}]}{[a_{i}\,,a^{\prime}_{i}]}{[a_{i}\,,a^{\prime}_{i}]}
lies in the
(5\alpha/2)
-neighborhood of
\gamma_{i}
, hence of
g_{i}\gamma
.
Recall that
\operatorname{diam}\mathchoice{\left(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma)\right)}{(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma))}{(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma))}{(\pi_{\gamma}(g\gamma))}\leqslant\alpha
whenever
g\notin E(h)
, see
(H3)
.
In view of
Lemma 2.4
is suffices to show that the diameter of
\mathchoice{\left[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}\right]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}\cap\mathchoice{\left[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}\right]}{[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}]}{[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}]}{[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}]}
is larger than
15\alpha
.

We first claim that
a^{\prime}_{i}-a_{i}>15\alpha
.
Indeed, it follows from
Proposition 3.18

(i)
that
\mathchoice{d\left(x_{i},q_{i}\right)}{d(x_{i},q_{i})}{d(x_{i},q_{i})}{d(x_{i},q_{i})}\leqslant r_{i}+7\alpha
and
\mathchoice{d\left(x^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}_{i}\right)}{d(x^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}{d(x^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}{d(x^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}\leqslant r_{i}+7\alpha
.
Thus the triangle inequality combined with (
8
) yields

a^{\prime}_{i}-a_{i}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(q_{i},q^{\prime}_{i}\right)}{d(q_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}{d(q_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}{d(q_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})}-10\alpha\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\right)}{d(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})}{d(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})}{d(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})}-2r_{i}-24\alpha>15\alpha.

Suppose now that the diameter of
\mathchoice{\left[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}\right]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}\cap\mathchoice{\left[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}\right]}{[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}]}{[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}]}{[a_{2}\,,a^{\prime}_{2}]}
is at most
15\alpha
.
Either
a^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant a_{2}+15\alpha
or
a^{\prime}_{2}\leqslant a_{1}+15\alpha
.
In the remainder of the proof, we assume that
a^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant a_{2}+15\alpha
(the other case works in the same way).
As we already observed,
\nu
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}\right]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}
lies in the
(5\alpha/2)
-neighborhood of
\gamma_{1}
, which contains
g_{1}o
.
It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 5.1]
that
g_{1}o
is
5\alpha
-close to the point
\nu(b_{1})
for some
b_{1}\in\mathchoice{\left[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}\right]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}{[a_{1}\,,a^{\prime}_{1}]}
.
However the triangle inequality gives

a^{\prime}_{1}-b_{1}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(q^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o\right)}{d(q^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o)}{d(q^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o)}{d(q^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o)}-10\alpha\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o\right)}{d(x^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o)}{d(x^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o)}{d(x^{\prime}_{1},g_{1}o)}-(r_{1}+17\alpha)\geqslant L_{1}-(r_{1}+17\alpha)>15\alpha

Consequently

b_{1}<a^{\prime}_{1}-15\alpha\leqslant a_{2}.

Recall that the distance between
\nu
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[b_{1}\,,a_{2}\right]}{[b_{1}\,,a_{2}]}{[b_{1}\,,a_{2}]}{[b_{1}\,,a_{2}]}
and
\gamma_{2}
is
\alpha
.
It follows from
Lemma 3.9
that the projection of
\nu(b_{1})
on
\gamma_{2}
is
4\alpha
-close to
q_{2}
.
Since projection on
\gamma_{2}
is large-scale Lipschitz (
Remark 2.1
) the projection
p^{\prime}
of
g_{1}o
on
\gamma_{2}
is
13\alpha
-close to
q_{2}
.
Recall that
p
is a projection of
g_{1}o
on
g_{2}\gamma
(and not just on
\gamma_{2}
).
However since
\mathchoice{d\left(p,g_{2}o\right)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}\leqslant L_{2}
, it belongs to
\gamma_{2}
.
Hence
p
is also a projection of
g_{1}o
on
\gamma_{2}
.
As
\gamma_{2}
is
\alpha
-contracting, we get
\mathchoice{d\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}{d(p,p^{\prime})}\leqslant 2\alpha
.
It follows now from the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle L_{2}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x_{2},g_{2}o\right)}{d(x_{2},g_{2}o)}{d(x_{2},g_{2}o)}{d(x_{2},g_{2}o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x_{2},q_{2}\right)}{d(x_{2},q_{2})}{d(x_{2},q_{2})}{d(x_{2},q_{2})}+\mathchoice{d\left(q_{2},p^{\prime}\right)}{d(q_{2},p^{\prime})}{d(q_{2},p^{\prime})}{d(q_{2},p^{\prime})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime},p\right)}{d(p^{\prime},p)}{d(p^{\prime},p)}{d(p^{\prime},p)}+\mathchoice{d\left(p,g_{2}o\right)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(p,g_{2}o\right)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}{d(p,g_{2}o)}+r_{2}+22\alpha.

This contradicts our assumption and the proof is complete. ∎

5.2
Admissible sequences

In the previous section, we defined a preferred radial limit set
\Lambda
with full measure (provided
G
is not virtually cyclic and the action of
G
on
X
is divergent).
We now begin our work to build a complete metric
d_{\infty}
on
\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim
.
Inspired by the case of hyperbolic groups, we start by defining a collections of ultra-metrics
(d_{L})
on
\Lambda/\negthickspace\sim
, which can be thought of as a “visual metrics on
\Lambda
seen from
o
”.

Vocabulary 5.2
.

We say that a subset
I\subset\mathbf{Z}
is
connected
if for all integers
i,j\in I
with
i\leqslant j
, the set
\llbracket i,j\rrbracket
is contained in
I
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
A sequence
(u_{i})
of elements of
G
indexed by a connected set
I\subset\mathbf{Z}
is
L
-admissible
if for every
i\in\mathbf{Z}
for which
i-1,i,i+1\in I
, there are
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
as well as
z\in B(u_{i-1}o,10\alpha)
and
z^{\prime}\in B(u_{i+1}o,10\alpha)
which are
(L,2\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
.
The number
\varepsilon_{i}
is called the
orientation
of
u_{i}
in the sequence.

If
I
is finite ( respectively
I=\mathbf{N}
) we say that the sequence
(u_{i})
if
finite
( respectively
infinite
).

Remark
.

Just as working with hyperbolic spaces involves many estimates in terms of the hyperbolicity constant
\delta
, our approach requires numerous computations dealing with errors in terms of
\alpha
.
In all the results below, we do not claim to have found the optimal estimates.
We sometimes rounded our calculations to get statements easier to digest.
What matters the most are the orders of magnitude, i.e. which quantities are large / small compare to
\alpha
.
∎

Being admissible is a
G
-invariant property: if
(u_{i})
is an
L
-admissible sequence, then so is
(gu_{i})
for every
g\in G
.
The definition is local (it only involves the indices
i-1
,
i
and
i+1
).
The next statement can be thought of as a local-to-global phenomenon.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
L>200\alpha
.
Assume that
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
is a finite
L
-admissible sequence.
Let
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
.
Let
\varepsilon_{i}
be the orientation of
u_{i}
in the sequence.
The points
u_{0}o
and
u_{n}o
are
(L,16\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{\left\langle u_{0}o,u_{n}o\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle u_{0}o,u_{n}o\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle u_{0}o,u_{n}o\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle u_{0}o,u_{n}o\rangle_{u_{i}o}}\leqslant 4\alpha
, for every
i\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
.
Moreover if
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
, then
u_{i}
belongs to
U(L-18\alpha)
.

Proof.

To make it easier to follow the computations we let
r=16\alpha
.
The first part of the statement is proved by induction on
n
.
If
n\leqslant 2
, it is a consequence of (
5
).
Let
n\geqslant 2
for which the statement holds.
Let
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n+1})
be finite
L
-admissible sequence.
For simplicity, for every
i\in\llbracket 0,n+1\rrbracket
, we let
y_{i}=u_{i}o
.
In addition, if
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, we write
\gamma_{i}\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
for the path given by
\gamma_{i}(t)=u_{i}\gamma(\varepsilon_{i}t)
and let
x_{i}=\gamma_{i}(-L)
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=\gamma_{i}(L)
.
According to the induction hypothesis applied with
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
and
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n+1})
we know that

(i)

y_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{x^{\prime}_{i}}(x_{i},r)
for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
, and

(ii)

y_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{x_{i}}(x^{\prime}_{i},r)
for every
i\in\llbracket 2,n\rrbracket
.

We are left to prove that
y_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{x^{\prime}_{n}}(x_{n},r)
and
y_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{x_{1}}(x^{\prime}_{1},r)
.
We focus on the latter property.
The other one follows by symmetry.
Denote by
p_{2}=\gamma_{2}(t_{2})
,
q_{2}=\gamma_{2}(s_{2})
, and
q^{\prime}_{2}=\gamma_{2}(s^{\prime}_{2})
respective projections of
x_{1}
,
y_{1}
, and
y_{n+1}
on
\gamma_{2}
.
Since
y_{n+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{x_{2}}(x^{\prime}_{2},r)
, we observe using
Lemma 2.3
that
s^{\prime}_{2}\geqslant L-(r+2\alpha)
.
Similarly,
s_{2}\leqslant-L+(r+2\alpha)
.
We now claim that
t_{2}\leqslant-L+(r+6\alpha)
.
Suppose on the contrary that it is not the case.
In particular,
t_{2}-s_{2}>4\alpha
, hence
\mathchoice{d\left(p_{2},q_{2}\right)}{d(p_{2},q_{2})}{d(p_{2},q_{2})}{d(p_{2},q_{2})}>4\alpha
.
Note that
p_{2}
and
q_{2}
are both projections onto
\gamma_{2}
of points from
\gamma_{1}
.
According to
(H3)
,
u_{1}^{-1}u_{2}\in E(h)
.
By
(H4)
, there is
\eta\in\{\pm 1\}
and
T\in\mathbf{R}
such that

\mathchoice{d\left(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\eta t+T)\right)}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\eta t+T))}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\eta t+T))}{d(\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma_{2}(\eta t+T))}\leqslant\alpha,\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(p_{2},x_{1}\right)}{d(p_{2},x_{1})}{d(p_{2},x_{1})}{d(p_{2},x_{1})}\leqslant\alpha
and
\mathchoice{d\left(q_{2},y_{1}\right)}{d(q_{2},y_{1})}{d(q_{2},y_{1})}{d(q_{2},y_{1})}\leqslant\alpha
, hence
\mathchoice{\left|t_{2}-(T-\eta L)\right|}{|t_{2}-(T-\eta L)|}{|t_{2}-(T-\eta L)|}{|t_{2}-(T-\eta L)|}\leqslant 2\alpha
and
\mathchoice{\left|s_{2}-T\right|}{|s_{2}-T|}{|s_{2}-T|}{|s_{2}-T|}\leqslant 2\alpha
.
We observed previously that
t_{2}-s_{2}>4\alpha
, which forces
\eta=-1
.
Consequently
x^{\prime}_{1}
is
\alpha
-close to the point
p^{\prime}_{2}=\gamma_{2}(t^{\prime}_{2})
where
t^{\prime}_{2}=-L+T
.
In particular,
t^{\prime}_{2}\leqslant s_{2}\leqslant t_{2}
, while
s_{2}\leqslant 0
.
It follows that

\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{1},y_{2}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}\geqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle p_{2},y_{2}\right\rangle_{p^{\prime}_{2}}}{\langle p_{2},y_{2}\rangle_{p^{\prime}_{2}}}{\langle p_{2},y_{2}\rangle_{p^{\prime}_{2}}}{\langle p_{2},y_{2}\rangle_{p^{\prime}_{2}}}-2\alpha\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(p^{\prime}_{2},q_{2}\right)}{d(p^{\prime}_{2},q_{2})}{d(p^{\prime}_{2},q_{2})}{d(p^{\prime}_{2},q_{2})}-2\alpha\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(x^{\prime}_{1},y_{1}\right)}{d(x^{\prime}_{1},y_{1})}{d(x^{\prime}_{1},y_{1})}{d(x^{\prime}_{1},y_{1})}-4\alpha\geqslant L-4\alpha>r

This contradicts the fact that
y_{2}
also belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{x_{1}}(x^{\prime}_{1},r)
and completes the proof of our claim.
Observe now, that
x_{1}
and
y_{n+1}
are
2\alpha
-separated by
(p_{2},q^{\prime}_{2})
.
According to
Proposition 3.18

(v)
,
\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\right\rangle_{y_{2}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{y_{2}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{y_{2}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{y_{2}}}\leqslant 4\alpha
.
Thus

\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{1},y_{2}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{1}}}+\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\right\rangle_{y_{2}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{y_{2}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{y_{2}}}{\langle x_{1},y_{n+1}\rangle_{y_{2}}}\leqslant 16\alpha\leqslant r,

(the middle inequality above follows from the definition of admissible chain).
This completes the proof of the induction step for
n+1
, and the first part of our statement.
The second part regarding Gromov products follows from
Proposition 3.18

(v)
.
The conclusion on
U
is an application of
Lemma 2.3
.
∎

The next two statements will help us defining the endpoints of an infinite admissible sequence.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
L>200\alpha
.
Let
n\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
.
Assume that
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
is a finite
L
-admissible sequence.
Then

\mathchoice{d\left(u_{n}o,u_{0}o\right)}{d(u_{n}o,u_{0}o)}{d(u_{n}o,u_{0}o)}{d(u_{n}o,u_{0}o)}\geqslant\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathchoice{d\left(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o\right)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o)}-8(n-1)\alpha\geqslant n(L-24\alpha).

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma 5.4
that
\mathchoice{\left\langle u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o\rangle_{u_{i}o}}\leqslant 4\alpha
, for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
, that is

\mathchoice{d\left(u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o\right)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{0}o)}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o\right)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o)}+\mathchoice{d\left(u_{i}o,u_{0}o\right)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{0}o)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{0}o)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{0}o)}-8\alpha.

Hence the first inequality.
We are left to prove that
\mathchoice{d\left(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o\right)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o)}{d(u_{i+1}o,u_{i}o)}\geqslant L-16\alpha
, for every
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
.
Assume first that
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
.
Denote by
\varepsilon_{i}
the orientation of
u_{i}
in the sequence.
Let
x_{i}=u_{i}\gamma(-\varepsilon_{i}L)
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i}\gamma(-\varepsilon_{i}L)
.
Combining
Lemma 5.4
and
Remark 4.3
we observe that

L-\mathchoice{d\left(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o\right)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o)}{d(u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o)}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,u_{i+1}o\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{i},u_{i+1}o\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle x_{i},u_{i+1}o\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle x_{i},u_{i+1}o\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle x_{i},u_{i+1}o\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}\leqslant 16\alpha,

whence our claim.
If
i=0
, the argument works in the same way switching the roles of
u_{i}
and
u_{i+1}
.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
L>200\alpha
.
Let
(u_{n})
be an infinite
L
-admissible sequence.
If
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are two accumulation points of
(u_{n}o)
then
\mathchoice{\left\|c_{1}-c_{2}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}\leqslant 20\alpha
.
Moreover any cocycle
c\in\partial X
equivalent to
c_{i}
satisfies
\mathchoice{\left\|c-c_{i}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c_{i}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c_{i}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c_{i}\|_{\infty}}\leqslant 20\alpha
.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that we are not claiming that
c_{i}
belongs to
\Lambda
.
Indeed, it certainly belongs to
\Lambda_{U}(o,4\alpha,L-18\alpha)
(see the proof below) but may fail to be in
\Lambda_{U}(o,4\alpha,L^{\prime})
for every
L^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
∎

Proof.

Let
k\in\mathbf{N}
.
According to
Lemma 5.4
, for every integer
n\geqslant k
, we have
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,u_{n}o\right\rangle_{u_{k}o}}{\langle o,u_{n}o\rangle_{u_{k}o}}{\langle o,u_{n}o\rangle_{u_{k}o}}{\langle o,u_{n}o\rangle_{u_{k}o}}\leqslant 4\alpha
.
Thus any two accumulation points
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
of
(u_{n}o)
, belong to
\mathcal{O}_{o}(u_{k}o,4\alpha)
.

Lemma 5.4
also tells us that
(o,u_{k}o)
has an
(\alpha,L-18\alpha)
-contracting tail.
Recall also that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,u_{k}o\right)}{d(o,u_{k}o)}{d(o,u_{k}o)}{d(o,u_{k}o)}
diverges to infinity (
Lemma 5.5
).
It follows now from
[
9
, Lemma 5.7]
that
\mathchoice{\left\|c_{1}-c_{2}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}{\|c_{1}-c_{2}\|_{\infty}}\leqslant 20\alpha
.
The above argument also proves that
c_{i}
belongs to
\Lambda_{U}(o,4\alpha,L-18\alpha)
.
The final conclusion is therefore a consequence of
[
9
, Lemma 5.10]
.
∎

Vocabulary 5.3
.

In view of the above statement, there exists
c\in\partial X
, such that any accumulation point of
(u_{n}o)
is equivalent to
c
.
This allows us to define the endpoints of an infinite
L
-admissible sequence
(u_{n})
, provided
L>200\alpha
.
Its
initial point
is
u_{0}
, while its
terminal point
is the equivalence class
[c]
of
c
.
We sometimes make an abuse of vocabulary and say that
c
is the endpoint of
(u_{n})
.
The initial and terminal points of a finite admissible sequence
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
are simply
u_{0}
and
u_{n}
.
Note that the endpoints of an admissible sequence are either elements of
G
or (equivalence classes of) cocycles in
\partial X
.

The next lemma generalizes
Lemma 5.4
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
L\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>200\alpha
.
Let
(u_{i})_{i\in\mathbf{N}}
be an infinite
L
-admissible sequence starting at
1
.
Let
c\in\partial X
be an endpoint of
(u_{i}o)
.
Let
i\in\mathbf{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
\varepsilon_{i}
be the orientation of
u_{i}
in the sequence.
Then
o
and
c
are
(L,36\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,c\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c\rangle_{u_{i}o}}\leqslant 4\alpha
.

Proof.

Assume first that
c^{*}\in\partial X
is an accumulation point of
(u_{i}o)
.
According to
Lemma 5.4
,
o
and
u_{j}o
are
(L,16\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
, for every
j>i
.
Passing to the limit, we get that
o
and
c^{*}
are still
(L,16\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
.
Let us now focus on
c
, which is by definition equivalent to
c^{*}
.
Thus
\mathchoice{\left\|c-c^{*}\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{*}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{*}\|_{\infty}}{\|c-c^{*}\|_{\infty}}\leqslant 20\alpha
.
It follows from (
5
) that
o
and
c
are
(L,36\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
.
The final conclusion is now a consequence of
Proposition 3.18
.
∎

We now prove the existence of infinite admissible sequences pointing to any point in
\Lambda
.
Our statement is actually slightly more general and provides a tool to extends admissible sequences.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
r,L,M\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>200\alpha
and
M>L+r+100\alpha
.
Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Let
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
be a finite
L
-admissible sequence.
Let
c\in\Lambda
.
If
u_{n-1}o
and
c
are
(M,r,\varepsilon)
-separated at
u_{n}
then the sequence
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
extends to an infinite
L
-admissible sequence from
1
to
[c]
.

Proof.

If
c
belongs to
\Lambda_{-}
( respectively
\Lambda_{+}
) we let
\varepsilon_{i}=-1
( respectively
\varepsilon_{i}=+1
) for every integer
i\geqslant n+1
.
For simplicity we let
L^{\prime}=L+13\alpha
.
Using
Proposition 4.13
, we build by induction a sequence
u_{n+1},u_{n+2},\dots
with the following property: for every
i\geqslant n+1
, the points
u_{i-1}o
and
c
are
(L^{\prime},2\alpha,\varepsilon_{i})
-separated at
u_{i}
.
Combining our assumption with
Remark 5.2
we know that
u_{n-1}o
and
c
are also
(L^{\prime},2\alpha,\varepsilon_{n})
-separated at
u_{n}
, where
\varepsilon_{n}=\varepsilon
.
We claim that
(u_{i})
is
L
-admissible.

For simplicity, we now write
y_{i}=u_{i}o
, for every
i\in\mathbf{N}
.
If
i\geqslant n
we also let
x_{i}=u_{i}\gamma(-\varepsilon_{i}L)
and
x^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i}\gamma(\varepsilon_{i}L)
.
It follows from
Proposition 3.18
that

\mathchoice{\left\langle y_{i-1},x^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle_{x_{i}}}{\langle y_{i-1},x^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{x_{i}}}{\langle y_{i-1},x^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{x_{i}}}{\langle y_{i-1},x^{\prime}_{i}\rangle_{x_{i}}}\leqslant 2\alpha,\quad\forall i\geqslant n.

Since
(u_{0},\dots,u_{n})
is already
L
-admissible, we are left to prove that

\mathchoice{\left\langle y_{i+1},x_{i}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle y_{i+1},x_{i}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle y_{i+1},x_{i}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle y_{i+1},x_{i}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}\leqslant 2\alpha,\quad\forall i\geqslant n.

Fix
i\geqslant n
.
Denote by
\gamma_{i}\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
the path given by
\gamma_{i}(t)=u_{i}\gamma(\varepsilon_{i}t)
.
Let
p=\gamma_{i}(s)
and
q=\gamma_{i}(t)
be respective projections of
y_{i+1}
and
c
onto
\gamma_{i}
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[0\,,L^{\prime}\right]}{[0\,,L^{\prime}]}{[0\,,L^{\prime}]}{[0\,,L^{\prime}]}
.
According to
Lemma 3.15
,
\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i},q\right)}{d(y_{i},q)}{d(y_{i},q)}{d(y_{i},q)}\geqslant L^{\prime}-9\alpha
.
We claim that
\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i},p\right)}{d(y_{i},p)}{d(y_{i},p)}{d(y_{i},p)}\geqslant L^{\prime}-13\alpha
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\mathchoice{d\left(p,q\right)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}{d(p,q)}>4\alpha
.
It follows from
Lemma 3.9
that
\mathchoice{\left\langle y_{i},c\right\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}{\langle y_{i},c\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}{\langle y_{i},c\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}{\langle y_{i},c\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i+1},p\right)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}-4\alpha
.
Recall that
y_{i}
and
c
are
(L^{\prime},2\alpha,\varepsilon_{i+1})
-separated at
u_{i+1}
.
Thus

\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i},y_{i+1}\right)}{d(y_{i},y_{i+1})}{d(y_{i},y_{i+1})}{d(y_{i},y_{i+1})}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}\right)}{d(y_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})}{d(y_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})}{d(y_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})}-\mathchoice{\left\langle y_{i},y_{i+1}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle y_{i},y_{i+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle y_{i},y_{i+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle y_{i},y_{i+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}\geqslant L^{\prime}-2\alpha

and
\mathchoice{\left\langle y_{i},c\right\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}{\langle y_{i},c\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}{\langle y_{i},c\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}{\langle y_{i},c\rangle_{y_{i+1}}}\leqslant 4\alpha
(
Proposition 3.18
).
Consequently
\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i+1},p\right)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}\leqslant 8\alpha
.
The triangle inequality now yields

\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i},p\right)}{d(y_{i},p)}{d(y_{i},p)}{d(y_{i},p)}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i},y_{i+1}\right)}{d(y_{i},y_{i+1})}{d(y_{i},y_{i+1})}{d(y_{i},y_{i+1})}-\mathchoice{d\left(y_{i+1},p\right)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}{d(y_{i+1},p)}\geqslant L^{\prime}-10\alpha.

which complete the proof of our claim.
Recall that
L^{\prime}-13\alpha\geqslant L
.
It follows from the previous discussion that
x_{i}
,
y_{i}
,
x^{\prime}_{i}
and
p
are aligned in this way on
u_{i}\gamma
.
Hence
\mathchoice{\left\langle x_{i},y_{i+1}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle x_{i},y_{i+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle x_{i},y_{i+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}{\langle x_{i},y_{i+1}\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{i}}}\leqslant 2\alpha
(
Lemma 2.3
).

We have shown that
(u_{i})
is an
L
-admissible sequence.
We are left to prove that its endpoint is indeed
[c]
.
Let
j\geqslant n+1
.
According to our construction,
c\in\mathcal{O}_{x_{j}}(x^{\prime}_{j},2\alpha)
(see
Remark 5.2
).
Since
(u_{i})
is a
L
-admissible sequence,
(o,u_{j}o)
has an
(\alpha,L-18\alpha)
-contracting tail and
o\in\mathcal{O}_{x^{\prime}_{j}}(x_{j},16\alpha)
(
Lemma 5.4
)
In other words,
o
and
c
are
(L,16\alpha,\varepsilon_{j})
-separated at
u_{j}
.
In particular,
c\in\mathcal{O}_{o}(y_{j},4\alpha)
.
As we noticed in the proof of
Lemma 5.6
, any accumulation point
b\in\partial X
of
(u_{i}o)
also belongs to
\mathcal{O}_{o}(y_{j},4\alpha)
.
It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 5.7]
that
\mathchoice{\left\|c-b\right\|_{\infty}}{\|c-b\|_{\infty}}{\|c-b\|_{\infty}}{\|c-b\|_{\infty}}\leqslant 20\alpha
, whence the result.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
L>200\alpha
.
For every
c\in\Lambda
, there is a an infinite
L
-admissible sequence from
1
to
[c]
.

Proof.

Fix
M>L+202\alpha
.
Let
u_{0}=1
.
Recall that
c
belongs to
\Lambda_{+}\cup\Lambda_{-}
.
According to
Proposition 4.13
there is
u_{1}\in G
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
o
and
c
are
(M,2\alpha,\varepsilon)
-separated at
u_{1}
.
Note the
(u_{0},u_{1})
is an
L
-admissible sequence (the condition to check is void).
Hence we can extend it to an infinite
L
-admissible sequence ending at
[c]
(
Proposition 5.8
).
∎

5.3
Cylinders

Definition 5.10
.

Let
L>200\alpha
.
Let
c\in\Lambda
.
The
L
-cylinder
of
c
denoted by
C_{L}(c)
is the set of all elements
u\in G
which belong to an
L
-admissible sequence from
1
to
[c]
.

Remark 5.4
.

Note that we have chosen
L
so that all the above statements hold.
In particular, the endpoints of any
L
-admissible sequence are well-defined.
∎

The next (technical) statement is one of the most crucial in our construction.
If
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are two cocycles which are “sufficiently close”, It will allow us to reroute the beginning of the cylinder of
c_{1}
to the cylinder of
c_{2}
.
Recall that
B_{G}(R)
stands for the ball of radius
R
centered at
1
in
G
for the metric induced by
X
, see (
1
).

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
r,L,M\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
with
L>200\alpha
and
M>2L+r+500\alpha
.
Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Let
c_{1},c_{2}\in\Lambda
.
Let
g\in G
.
Suppose that
o
and
\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
are
(M,r,\varepsilon)
-separated at
g
.
Then
C_{L}(c_{1})
and
C_{L}(c_{2})
coincide on
B_{G}(R)
where
R=\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}
.

Proof.

We prove that
C_{L}(c_{1})\cap B_{G}(R)
is contained in
C_{L}(c_{2})
.
The other inclusion follows by symmetry.
Let
u\in C_{L}(c_{1})
such that
\mathchoice{d\left(o,uo\right)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}{d(o,uo)}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}
.
By definition,
u
belongs to an infinite
L
-admissible sequence
(u_{i})
from
1
to
[c_{1}]
.

For every
i\in\mathbf{N}
, we write
p_{i}=g\gamma(\varepsilon t_{i})
for a projection of
u_{i}o
on
g\gamma
.
Since
o
and
c
are
(M,r,\varepsilon)
-separated at
g
we know that
t_{0}\leqslant-M+(r+2\alpha)
(
Lemma 2.3
).
Let
q_{k}=\gamma(\varepsilon s_{k})
be a projection of
c_{k}
on
\gamma
restricted to
\mathchoice{\left[-M\,,M\right]}{[-M\,,M]}{[-M\,,M]}{[-M\,,M]}
.
It follows from
Proposition 3.18
that
s_{k}\geqslant M-(r+7\alpha)
.

Claim 5.12
.

Let
i\in\mathbf{N}
.

—

If
t_{i}>t_{0}+\alpha
, then
\mathchoice{d\left(o,u_{i}o\right)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}+t_{i}-6\alpha
.

—

If
t_{i}<s_{1}-4\alpha
, then
\mathchoice{d\left(o,u_{i}o\right)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}+t_{i}+10\alpha
.

Suppose first that
t_{i}>t_{0}+\alpha
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(p_{0},p_{i}\right)}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}>\alpha
.
It follows then from
Lemma 2.3
that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{d\left(o,u_{i}o\right)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{0}\right)}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p_{0},p_{i}\right)}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}-6\alpha

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{0}\right)}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}+(t_{i}-t_{0})-6\alpha

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{0}\right)}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p_{0},go\right)}{d(p_{0},go)}{d(p_{0},go)}{d(p_{0},go)}+t_{i}-6\alpha

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}+t_{i}-6\alpha.

Suppose now that
t_{i}<s_{1}-4\alpha
.
In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(p_{i},q_{1}\right)}{d(p_{i},q_{1})}{d(p_{i},q_{1})}{d(p_{i},q_{1})}>4\alpha
.
Note that

\mathchoice{\left\langle o,p_{i}\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}+\mathchoice{\left\langle o,c_{1}\right\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}=\mathchoice{\left\langle o,c_{1}\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}+\mathchoice{\left\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\right\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}},

(It suffices to unwrap the Gromov product).
However
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,c_{1}\right\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}\geqslant 0
, while
\mathchoice{\left\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\right\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}{\langle u_{i}o,c_{1}\rangle_{p_{i}}}\leqslant 2\alpha
(
Lemma 3.9
).
Hence

\mathchoice{\left\langle o,p_{i}\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle o,c_{1}\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,c_{1}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}+2\alpha.

Since
(u_{i})
is an
L
-admissible sequence ending at
[c_{1}]
, the Gromov product on the right hand side is at most
4\alpha
(
Lemma 5.4
).
It follows then from the triangle inequality that

\mathchoice{d\left(o,u_{i}o\right)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}{d(o,u_{i}o)}-\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{i}\right)}{d(o,p_{i})}{d(o,p_{i})}{d(o,p_{i})}\leqslant\mathchoice{\left\langle o,p_{i}\right\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}{\langle o,p_{i}\rangle_{u_{i}o}}\leqslant 6\alpha.

Applying
Lemma 2.3
, we know that
\mathchoice{\left\langle o,go\right\rangle_{p_{0}}}{\langle o,go\rangle_{p_{0}}}{\langle o,go\rangle_{p_{0}}}{\langle o,go\rangle_{p_{0}}}\leqslant 2\alpha
.
Consequently

\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{i}\right)}{d(o,p_{i})}{d(o,p_{i})}{d(o,p_{i})}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{0}\right)}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p_{0},p_{i}\right)}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}{d(p_{0},p_{i})}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,p_{0}\right)}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}{d(o,p_{0})}+\mathchoice{d\left(p_{0},go\right)}{d(p_{0},go)}{d(p_{0},go)}{d(p_{0},go)}+t_{i}\leqslant\mathchoice{d\left(o,go\right)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}{d(o,go)}+t_{i}+4\alpha.

Combined with the previous inequality, it completes the proof of our claim.

We denote by
j
the largest integer such that
t_{j}\leqslant 40\alpha
.
Since
(u_{i}o)
diverges to infinity (
Lemma 5.5
) the existence of such an integer
j
is ensured by our previous claim.
It also implies that
u=u_{i}
for some
i\leqslant j
.
We are going to build an element
g^{\prime}\in g\left\langle h\right\rangle
such that
u_{j}o
and
c_{2}
are “sufficiently separated” at
g^{\prime}
while
(u_{0},\dots,u_{j},g^{\prime})
is still an
L
-admissible sequence.
Denote by
\varepsilon_{j}
the orientation of
u_{j}
in
(u_{i})
.
For simplicity, we write
\gamma_{j}\colon\mathbf{R}\to X
for the path defined by
\gamma_{j}(t)=u_{j}\gamma(\varepsilon_{j}t)
.
Set
x_{j}=\gamma_{j}(-L)
and
x^{\prime}_{j}=\gamma_{j}(L)
.
We now distinguish two cases.

Case 1.

Suppose that
u_{j}^{-1}g
belongs to
E(h)
.

By
(H4)
there is
\eta\in\{\pm 1\}
and
T_{j}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that

\mathchoice{d\left(\gamma_{j}(\eta t),g\gamma(\varepsilon t+\varepsilon T_{j})\right)}{d(\gamma_{j}(\eta t),g\gamma(\varepsilon t+\varepsilon T_{j}))}{d(\gamma_{j}(\eta t),g\gamma(\varepsilon t+\varepsilon T_{j}))}{d(\gamma_{j}(\eta t),g\gamma(\varepsilon t+\varepsilon T_{j}))}\leqslant\alpha,\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

(9)

In particular,
\mathchoice{d\left(u_{j}o,p_{j}\right)}{d(u_{j}o,p_{j})}{d(u_{j}o,p_{j})}{d(u_{j}o,p_{j})}\leqslant\alpha
and thus
\mathchoice{\left|t_{j}-T_{j}\right|}{|t_{j}-T_{j}|}{|t_{j}-T_{j}|}{|t_{j}-T_{j}|}\leqslant 2\alpha
.
Recall that
q_{1}=\gamma(\varepsilon s_{1})
, which is a projection of
c_{1}
on
\gamma
, satisfies
s_{1}\geqslant M-(r+7\alpha)
while
c_{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{x_{j}}(x^{\prime}_{j},36\alpha)
, see
Lemma 5.7
.
This forces
\eta=1
.
Set
T=t_{j}+L+150\alpha
.
In view of
(H5)
, there is
g^{\prime}\in g\left\langle h\right\rangle
such that

\mathchoice{d\left(g^{\prime}\gamma(t-\varepsilon T),g\gamma(t)\right)}{d(g^{\prime}\gamma(t-\varepsilon T),g\gamma(t))}{d(g^{\prime}\gamma(t-\varepsilon T),g\gamma(t))}{d(g^{\prime}\gamma(t-\varepsilon T),g\gamma(t))}\leqslant\alpha,\quad\forall t\in\mathbf{R}.

(10)

Let
